Proof:
See Appendix
B.2
.

Similar to the restrictions in the existing results for soft interventions, the identifiability results for hard interventions in the existing literature restrict the latent causal model, e.g., to linear causal models
(
Squires et al. 2023
;
Buchholz et al. 2023
)
. In contrast, Theorem
16
does not impose any restriction on the latent causal model and shows that one stochastic hard intervention per node is sufficient for the identifiability of general latent causal models.

Finally, we comment on the differences between the identifiability results for soft and hard interventions under missing intervention targets and discuss the identifiability of latent subgraphs under special cases.

Remark 0
.

Let
\mathcal{J}\subset[n]
denote a non-complete set of intervention targets, i.e.,
\mathcal{J}\neq[n]
. We note the immediate implications of our results as follows.

1.

Partial identifiability of latent variables.
Theorem
11
immediately implies that for all
i\in\mathcal{J}
we can recover
Z_{i}
up to mixing with parents. However, the unmixing procedure for hard interventions resolves the mixing with parents sequentially. Specifically, to identify
Z_{i}
up to scaling, we use the fact that the parents of
Z_{i}
are already identified up to scaling. Therefore, even if we have a hard intervention on
Z_{i}
, we cannot identify
Z_{i}
up to scaling consistency when its parents are not identified up to scaling.

2.

Ancestrally closed set of interventions.
Let
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}
denote the induced subgraph of
\mathcal{G}
over the set of nodes
\mathcal{J}
. If
\mathcal{J}
is not ancestrally closed, i.e.,
{\rm{pa}}(\mathcal{J})\not\subseteq\mathcal{J}
, then the marginal distribution of
Z_{\mathcal{J}}
is not a causally sufficient model. Conversely, if
\mathcal{J}
is an ancestrally closed set, then the marginal of
Z_{\mathcal{J}}
comes from a causally sufficient model with graph
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}
. Subsequently, using Stage L2 of Algorithm 1 for score differences of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\mathcal{J}}
, we obtain the transitive closure of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}
. Finally, performing the unmixing procedure over the ancestrally closed set of
hard
interventions, Stage L3 of Algorithm 1 ensures the perfect recovery of
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{J}}
.

5.4
Identifiability up to Surrounding Parents

For linear transformations, we finally investigate the conditions under which soft interventions are guaranteed to achieve identifiability results stronger than transitive closure and mixing up to parents. In particular, we specify one condition on the rank of the score function differences
\{{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}:m\in[n]\}
, formalized next.

Assumption 2

(Full-rank Score Difference)
.

For all interventional environments
\mathcal{E}^{m}\in\mathcal{E}
we have

\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})=|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})|\ .

(60)

For insight into this assumption, it can be readily verified that for linear Gaussian latent models
\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})\leq 2
and on the other hand, for sufficiently nonlinear causal models,
\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})
is
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})
. This assumption is stronger than Assumption
1
since it implies that the effects of an intervention on
all
parents of the target variable are different. We will provide more discussions on this assumption in Section
5.5
. Under this condition, Algorithm
2
identifies the latent graph and the latent variables with the following key intuitions.

Latent graph estimation.

Recall that
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}=[\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}]^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\cdot\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}
. Since
\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}
has full row-rank
n
, Assumption
2
implies that
\rank(\bR_{\bX}^{m})=|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})|
for all
m\in[n]
, which further implies that Lemma
10
becomes an equality as well, i.e.,

{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m})={\rm span}\big\{[\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}_{i}]^{\top}\,:\,i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})\big\}\ .

(61)

Then, for any
t,k\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{t})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})

\displaystyle={\rm span}\big\{[\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}_{i}]^{\top}\,:\,i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{t})\cap\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{k})\big\}\ ,

(62)

\displaystyle\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{t})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})\big)

\displaystyle=|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{t})\cap\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{k})|

(63)

\displaystyle=|{\rm{pa}}(I^{t})\cap{\rm{pa}}(I^{k})|+\mathds{1}\big(\{I^{t}\in{\rm{pa}}(I^{k})\}\lor\{I^{k}\in{\rm{pa}}(I^{t})\}\big)\ .

(64)

In Algorithm
2
, we first obtain a topological order of the true DAG using the graph estimate from Algorithm
1
and initialize the new estimate
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
with the empty graph. Consider a pair
(t,k)
such that
I^{t}\in{\rm{pa}}(I^{k})
. If
\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)
does not contain node
t
in
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
, then by (
64
) we have
\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{t})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})\big)>|\hat{\rm{pa}}(t)\cap\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)|
. We use this observation to sequentially identify the parents of each node, starting from root node(s) and gradually advancing to leaf node(s). In the end, resulting
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
equals
\mathcal{G}
under permutation
\mathcal{I}
.

Algorithm 2

LSCALE-I for sufficiently nonlinear latent causal models

1:

Input:

\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}
for all
m\in[n]
, topological order
\pi
from Algorithm
1

2:

Latent graph estimation

3:

Initialize
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
with empty graph

4:

for

k\in(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{n})

do

5:

for

t\in(\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{k-1})

do

6:

if

\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{t})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})\big)>|\hat{\rm{pa}}(t)\cap\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)|

then

7:

Update
\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)\leftarrow\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)\cup\{t\}

8:

end

if

9:

end

for

10:

end

for

11:

Encoder estimation

12:

Initialize
\hat{\mathbf{H}}\leftarrow\mathbf{0}_{n\times d}

13:

for

m\in(1,\dots,n)

do

14:

Select any
\mathbf{h}\in\!\!\!\textstyle\bigcap\limits_{k\,\in\,\hat{\rm{ch}}(m)\,\cup\,m}\!\!\!{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})

15:

\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}\leftarrow\mathbf{h}^{\top}

16:

end

for

17:

Return

\hat{\mathcal{G}}
,
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
, and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}

Encoder estimation.

In Algoritm
2
, we refine the encoder estimation step of Algorithm
1
as follows. Extending (
62
) and using the graph recovery result, we have

\displaystyle\textstyle\bigcap\limits_{k\,\in\,\hat{\rm{ch}}(m)\,\cup\,m}{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})

\displaystyle={\rm span}\big\{[\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}_{i}]^{\top}\,:\,i\in\textstyle\bigcap\limits_{k\,:\,I^{k}\in\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(I^{m})}\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{k})\big\}\ .

(65)

Now, consider
I^{t}\notin{\rm{sur}}(I^{m})
. By the definition of surrounding parents, there exists
I^{j}\in\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(I^{m})
such that
I^{j}\notin{\rm{ch}}(I^{t})
. Then,
I^{j}
is not in the intersection of the parent sets in the right-hand-side of (
65
), which means
[\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}_{j}]^{\top}
is not in the column space of the left-hand-side of (
65
). Therefore, by choosing
\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}
from this column space, we obtain for all
m\in[n]
,

\displaystyle[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}})]_{m}=\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}\cdot\mathbf{X}

\displaystyle=c_{\ell}\cdot Z_{\ell}+\sum_{i\in{\rm{sur}}(\ell)}c_{i}\cdot Z_{i}\ ,\qquad\mbox{where}\;\ell=I^{m}\ ,

(66)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}})

\displaystyle=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm pa}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,

(67)

where
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
is the permutation matrix of the intervention order
(I^{1},\dots,I^{n})
,
\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}
has non-zero diagonal entries and satisfies
\big[\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}\big]_{i,j}=0
for all
j\notin\overline{{\rm{sur}}}(i)
.
\square

Theorem 18

(Linear – Soft Interventions for Nonlinear SCMs)
.

Under Assumption
2
for linear transformations, using observational data and interventional data from one
soft
intervention per node suffice to identify (i) the latent DAG
\mathcal{G}
perfectly, and (ii) the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
with consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents, and recovered latent variables satisfy Markov property with respect to
\mathcal{G}
. Specifically, Algorithm
2
achieves these identifiability guarantees.

Proof:

Proof of consistency of latent variables up to mixing with surrounding parents is given above in complete detail. For the proof of perfect graph recovery and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
satisfies Markov property with respect to
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
, see Appendix
B.3
.

\square

Theorem
18
has two important implications. First, the latent DAG can be identified using
only
soft interventions under mild nonlinearity assumptions on the latent causal model. To our knowledge, this is the first result in the literature for fully recovering latent DAG with soft interventions without restricting the graphical structure, e.g.,
Zhang et al. 2023
require
linear faithfulness
assumption to achieve similar results, which is only shown to hold for nonlinear latent models with polytree structure. Secondly, the estimated latent variables reveal the true conditional independence relationships since they satisfy the Markov property with respect to the estimated latent graph, which is isomorphic to the latent graph. Recalling that the motivation of CRL is learning useful representations that preserve causal relationships, our result shows that it can be achieved without perfect identifiability for a large class of models. Furthermore,
Jin and Syrgkanis 2023
establish that under some nondegeneracy assumptions, mixing consistency up to surrounding parents is the best possible result when using single-node soft interventions. Hence, results in Theorem
18
are tight for the considered setting.

5.5
Discussion on Assumptions
1
and
2

In this subsection, we elaborate on Assumptions
1
and
2
, which are relevant to Theorems
14
and
18
, respectively. Assumption
1
essentially states that score changes in the coordinates of the intervened node and a parent of the intervened node are linearly independent. This property holds for (but is not limited to) the widely adopted additive noise models specified in (
7
) when we apply hard interventions.

Lemma 0
.

Assumption
1
is satisfied for additive noise models under hard interventions.

Proof:
See Appendix
D.1
.

When soft interventions are applied, the transitive closure of
\mathcal{G}
cannot be identified without making assumptions about the effect of the interventions. Specifically, for linear latent causal models,
Buchholz et al. 2023
prove impossibility results for pure shift interventions and
Squires et al. 2023
show that a
genericity condition
is necessary for identifying the transitive closure of the latent DAG. Therefore, Assumption
1
can be interpreted as the counterpart of the commonly adopted assumptions in the literature on soft interventions adapted to the setting of general latent causal models. Finally, the next example demonstrates the working of Assumption
1
on a linear Gaussian latent model.

Example 0
.

Consider a linear Gaussian latent model with
\mathbf{Z}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
. Score function of
\mathbf{Z}
is given by
{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})=-\Sigma^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}
. Let
Z_{i}=\mathbf{w}\cdot\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}+N_{i}
where
N_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{i}^{2})
for the node
i
. Consider an intervention on node
i
on environment
\mathcal{E}^{m}
such that
Z^{m}_{i}=\bar{\mathbf{w}}\cdot\mathbf{Z}^{m}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}+\bar{N}_{i}
, where
Z_{m}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\Sigma})
, and
\bar{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2})
, which yields
{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})=-(\Sigma^{-1}-\bar{\Sigma}^{-1})\cdot\mathbf{z}
. Then, for a node
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
, we obtain

\displaystyle[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\frac{1}{\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)z_{i}-\left(\frac{\mathbf{w}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\frac{\bar{\mathbf{w}}}{\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\ ,

(68)

\displaystyle[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{w_{k}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\frac{\bar{w}_{k}}{\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)z_{i}+\left(w_{k}\frac{\mathbf{w}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}-\bar{w}_{k}\frac{\bar{\mathbf{w}}}{\bar{\sigma}_{i}^{2}}\right)\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\ ,

(69)

where
w_{k}
and
\bar{w}_{k}
correspond to weights of the parent
Z_{k}
in observational and interventional models, respectively. Note that, for Assumption
1
to be violated, there must exists a constant
\kappa\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\kappa\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}=\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{k}\ ,\quad\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ .

(70)

However, using (
68
) and (
69
), this is possible if and only if
w_{k}=\bar{w}_{k}
. Therefore, if the weight of the node
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
changes, Assumption
1
is satisfied for the node pair
(i,k)
.

Given the known result that perfect identifiability is impossible for linear Gaussian models given soft interventions
(
Squires et al. 2023
)
, the purpose of Assumption
2
is to get more insight into the extent of identifiability guarantees under soft interventions. Intuitively, the mentioned impossibility results for linear Gaussian models are due to the rank deficiency of score differences for linear models – specifically, we know that
\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})\leq 2
for linear Gaussian models. In contrast, for sufficiently nonlinear causal models,
\rank(\bR_{\bZ}^{m})
can be as high as
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})
. Assumption
2
ensures that this upper bound is satisfied with equality for all nodes. This condition holds for the class of sufficiently nonlinear models, such as quadratic causal models. In particular, we show that this condition holds for the two-layer neural networks (NNs) as a function class that can effectively approximate any continuous function. This result is formalized in the next lemma.

Lemma 0
.

Consider the additive model in (
7
) where
f_{i}
is a two-layer NN with sigmoid activation function, and weight matrices
\mathbf{W}^{i}
and
\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i}
for observational and interventional mechanisms, respectively. If
\max\{\rank(\bW^i)\,,\,\rank(\bar\bW^i)\}=|{\rm{pa}}(i)|
for all
i\in[n]
, then Assumption
2
holds.

We discuss the nonlinearity and the proof of Lemma
21
in Appendix
D.2
.

6
CRL under General Transformations

In this setting, we consider general transformations without any parametric assumption for transformation
g
. In the previous section, we exploited the transformation’s linearity and recovered the true encoder’s parameters sequentially using
one
intervention per node. For general transformations (parametric or non-parametric), however, we cannot use the same parametric approach and rely on the properties of linear transforms. To rectify these and design the general CRL algorithm, we use more information in the form of
two
interventions per node. Specifically, we will present the steps of leveraging the score functions under two interventions and build an algorithm that will identify the true encoder and recover the true causal representations. The algorithm is referred to as
G
eneral
S
core-based
Ca
usal
L
atent
E
stimation via
I
nterventions (GSCALE-I), which will be summarized in Algorithm
3
. We also present additional results and discuss the role of various inputs and the distinction of our results compared to the existing literature.

Inputs.

The inputs of GSCALE-I are the observed data from the observational environment, the data from two interventional environments per node, whether environments are coupled/uncoupled, and a set of valid encoders
\mathcal{H}
. Two sets of interventional environments are denoted by
\mathcal{E}=\{\mathcal{E}^{1},\dots,\mathcal{E}^{n}\}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}=\{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{n}\}
, as defined in Section
3.3
. For these inputs, we compute the score functions
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}
,
\{{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{n}\}
and
\{\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1},\dots,\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{n}\}
. Note that we will use Lemma
8
again to ensure access to the latent score differences by using these observed score functions.

Statistical diversity.

For being more informative than a single intervention mechanism, we assume that the two intervention mechanisms per node are sufficiently distinct. This is formalized by defining
interventional discrepancy

(
Liang et al. 2023
)
among the causal mechanisms of a latent variable.

Definition 0

(Interventional Discrepancy)
.

Two intervention mechanisms with pdfs
p,q:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are said to satisfy
interventional discrepancy
if

\frac{\partial}{\partial u}\frac{p(u)}{q(u)}\neq 0\ ,\quad{\forall u\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\mathcal{T}}\ ,

(71)

where
\mathcal{T}
is a null set (i.e., has a zero Lebesgue measure).

This condition ensures that the two distributions are sufficiently distinct, formally expressed as the partial derivative of their ratio with respect to the intervened variable is nonzero almost everywhere. For instance, two univariate distinct Gaussians trivially satisfy this discrepancy condition. As shown by
Liang et al. 2023
, even when the latent graph
\mathcal{G}
is known, for identifiability via one intervention per node, it is necessary to have interventional discrepancy between observational distribution
p_{i}
and interventional distribution
q_{i}
, for all
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
.

6.1
Rationale of GSCALE-I and Partial Identifiability

Similarly to LSCALE-I, analyzing GSCALE-I involves leveraging score differences. However, in contrast to linear transformations, estimated and true latent score differences are not always related by a constant matrix for general transformations. To circumvent this issue, we rely on Lemma
7
(iii), i.e., the score difference of
coupled
interventional environments is one-sparse. As such, the key idea is looking for an encoder
h\in\mathcal{H}
that adheres to this sparsity structure, and we will show that such an encoder will ensure componentwise identifiability of the latent variables.
To formalize these, corresponding to each valid encoder
h\in\mathcal{H}
, we define the score change matrix
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)
with entries:

\displaystyle[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)]_{i,m}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\,\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_{i}\Big]\ ,

(72)

where expectations are under the measures of latent score functions induced by the probability measure of observational data. We also denote the
true
score change matrix under true encoder
g^{-1}
by
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\triangleq\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1})
with entries

[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}]_{i,m}\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\ ,\quad\forall i,m\in[n]\ .

(73)

For clarity in the exposition of the ideas, we consider coupled environments here, i.e.,
I^{m}=\tilde{I}^{m}
for all
m\in[n]
. Then, using (
33
) in Lemma
7
we have

[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}]_{i,m}\neq 0\;\;\iff\;\;i=I^{m}\ .

(74)

This implies that
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\}
is a permutation matrix,
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\top}
and

\mathds{1}[{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}}]_{:,m}=\mathbf{e}_{\ell}\ ,\quad\mbox{where}\;\ell=I^{m}\ ,

(75)

and
\mathbf{e}_{\ell}
denotes the
\ell
-th standard basis vector.
Subsequently, the key idea for identifying the encoder is that the changes between score estimates
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h)
and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};h)
should match the sparsity structure for those under the true encoder
g^{-1}
. In the next result, we leverage the sparsity structure in (
75
) for two interventions on the same node to identify an intervened variable, formalized as follows.

Theorem 23

(General – Node-level Identifiability)
.

Two hard interventions (with interventional discrepancy) on the same target node
I^{m}=\tilde{I}^{m}=\ell
suffices to recover
Z_{\ell}
up to a diffeomorphism. In particular, the following encoder

\hat{h}_{m}=\arg\min\limits_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\;\big\|[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)]_{:,m}-\mathbf{e}_{m}\big\|^{2}

(76)

satisfies
\big[\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{h}_{m})\big]_{m}=\phi_{\ell}(Z_{\ell})
for a diffeomorphism
\phi_{\ell}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
.

We will show shortly that given a complete set of coupled interventions, Theorem
23
will readily imply the identifiability of all variables (in a similar spirit to going from partial to complete identifiability in Section
5
). Furthermore, the results for uncoupled interventions will also leverage this key result. Hence, we provide the detailed proof of Theorem
23
as follows.

Proof:

We start by providing a direct result of Corollary
9
, which will be used in multiple instances in this section.
Consider an encoder
h\in\mathcal{H}
and an invertible matrix
\mathbf{A}\in{\sf GL}(n,{\mathbb{R}})
. Then, using Corollary
9
, score functions of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h)
and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\mathbf{A}\cdot h)
are related by

{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\mathbf{A}\cdot h)=\mathbf{A}^{-\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\ .

(77)

Recall that score change matrices are defined via the absolute value of the score differences. Therefore, (
77
) implies that for a positively scaled permutation matrix
\mathbf{A}
we have

\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\mathbf{A}\cdot h)=\mathbf{A}^{-\top}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)\ ,\quad\forall h\in\mathcal{H}\ .

(78)

Denote the interventional environments by
\mathcal{E}^{m}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{m}
with
I^{m}=\tilde{I}^{m}=\ell
. We show that the minimum value of (
76
) is zero as follows. Let
h^{*}=\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}^{\top}\cdot g^{-1}
. Recall that
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\top}
, and by definition,
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}
has only non-zero entries. Thus, using (
78
), we have
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\rm h^{*})=\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
, which makes the objective in (
76
) zero. Next, we show that
any
solution of (
76
) recovers
\hat{Z}_{\ell}
up to a diffeomorphism.

Consider encoder
\hat{h}_{m}\in\mathcal{H}
that satisfies
[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\hat{h}_{m})]_{:,m}=\mathbf{e}_{m}
. Let
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
denote
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{h}_{m})
and
f\triangleq\hat{h}_{m}\circ g
, so we have
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=f(\mathbf{Z})
and
\mathbf{Z}=f^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})
. Using score difference transformation property in (
40
) and one-sparse property of
[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})]
via Lemma
7
(iii), we have

\displaystyle[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\hat{h}_{m})]_{i,m}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\big|_{i}\Big]

(79)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{i}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]\big|\Big]

(80)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{\ell,i}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{\ell}\Big]\ .

(81)

By definition of
\hat{h}_{m}
,
[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\hat{h}_{m})]_{i,m}=0
for all
i\neq m
. Also, interventional discrepancy between
q_{\ell}(z_{l})
and
\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{l})
implies that
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell}\neq 0
except for a null set. Hence, if
\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell,i}
is non-zero over a non-zero-measure set within
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then
[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\hat{h}_{m})]_{i,m}
would not be zero. Therefore, we have
\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell,i}=0
except for a null set. Since
J_{f}^{-1}
is a continuous function, this implies that

\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell,i}=0\ ,\quad\forall i\in[n]\setminus m,\;\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(82)

Furthermore, since
J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})
must be invertible, we have
[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})]_{\ell,m}\neq 0
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Consider
Z_{\ell}=[f^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})]_{\ell}
. Since
J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})=J_{f^{-1}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}})
, (
82
) implies that
Z_{\ell}=\psi(\hat{Z}_{m})
for some diffeomorphism
\psi:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus,
\hat{Z}_{m}=\psi^{-1}(\hat{Z}_{\ell})
where
\ell=I^{m}
, which concludes the proof.

\square

6.2
Complete Identifiability under Coupled Interventions

After achieving the node-level latent variable identifiability from one pair of coupled interventions, we extend the objective function in Theorem
23
for a
complete
set of coupled interventions.

Encoder estimation.

In Stage G1 of Algorithm
3
, we combine the objectives in (
76
) for all
m\in[n]
and form our encoder estimate by solving the following optimization problem

\min\limits_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\ .

(83)

Recall that the valid encoders set
\mathcal{H}
consist of invertible encoders over the input space
\mathcal{X}
. Hence, (
83
) is readily equivalent to solving the following problem for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
:

\min\limits_{h}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\;+\;\lambda\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\|h^{-1}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{X}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]\ .

(84)

Lemma 0
.

Given a complete set of coupled interventions that satisfy interventional discrepancy, output
\hat{h}
of Algorithm
3
satisfies componentwise latent recovery.

Proof:

Following Theorem
23
for all
m\in[n]
immediately implies the desired result. First, recall that
h^{*}=\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}\cdot g^{-1}
satisfies
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*})=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
and belongs to
\mathcal{H}
. Therefore,
h^{*}
makes the objective (
84
) zero. Next, consider a solution
\hat{h}
that makes (
84
) zero. Since
\hat{h}
makes the reconstruction loss term zero, i.e., it has a valid inverse
\hat{h}^{-1}
,
\hat{h}
belongs to the set of valid encoders
\mathcal{H}
. Then, for all
m\in[n]
, we have
[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(\hat{h})]_{:,m}=\mathbf{e}_{m}
. By Theorem
23
, this implies

\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{h})=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\phi(\mathbf{Z})\ ,

(85)

where
\phi(\mathbf{Z})=(\phi_{1}(Z_{1}),\dots,\phi_{n}(Z_{n}))
is a componentwise diffeomorphism, which concludes the proof.

\square

Latent graph estimation.

Next, we use the encoder estimate
\hat{h}
for recovering the latent graph
\mathcal{G}
. In Stage G2 of Algorithm
3
, we use the score differences between observational and interventional environments and form
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
with parent sets

\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq m:{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\Big\}\ ,\quad\forall m\in[n]\ .

(86)

Lemma 0
.

Given a complete set of coupled interventions that satisfy interventional discrepancy, Algorithm
3
output
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and true latent DAG
\mathcal{G}
are related through a graph isomorphism.

Proof:

Denote
f=\hat{h}\circ g
, so we have
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=f(\mathbf{Z})
. Using the score transform between
\mathbf{Z}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
and the structure of
J_{f}^{-1}
given in (
82
), we have

\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{i}=\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{i,I^{i}}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{i}\Big|

(87)

Since
[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{i,I^{i}}\neq 0
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, using Lemma
7
(i), we find

i\in\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\;\;\iff\;\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{i}}\Big]\neq 0\;\;\iff\;\;I^{i}\in{\rm{pa}}(I^{m}),

(88)

which concludes the proof that
\mathcal{G}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
are related through a graph isomorphism.

\square

Algorithm 3

G
eneralized
S
core-based
Ca
usal
L
atent
E
stimation via
I
nterventions (GSCALE-I)

1:

Input:

\mathcal{H}
, samples of
\mathbf{X}
from environment
\mathcal{E}^{0}
and coupled environments
\{(\mathcal{E}^{m},\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{m}):m\in[n]\}

2:

Compute score functions:
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}},{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}
, and
\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}
for all
m\in[n]
.

3:

4:

Stage G1: Encoder estimation
:

\hat{h}\leftarrow\arg\min\limits_{h}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\,+\,\lambda\,{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\|h^{-1}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{X}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]

5:

Latent variable estimates:
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=\hat{h}(\mathbf{X})
.

6:

7:

Stage G2: Latent graph estimation

8:

Construct latent DAG estimate
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
with parent sets

\hat{{\rm{pa}}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq m:{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\Big\}\ ,\quad\forall m\in[n]\ .

9:

return

\hat{\mathcal{G}}
,
\hat{h}
, and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}

Theorem 26

(General – Coupled Environments)
.

Using observational data and interventional data from two
coupled
hard environments for which the pair
(q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})
satisfies interventional discrepancy for all
i\in[n]
, suffice to identify (i) the latent DAG
\mathcal{G}
perfectly and (ii) the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
up to componentwise diffeomorphisms specified in Definition
5
. Specifically, Algorithm
3
achieves these identifiability guarantees.

Proof:

Combining Lemma
24
and Lemma
25
gives the theorem statements.

\square

We emphasize that a key contribution of Theorem
26
beyond the identifiability result is that it provides a well-defined objective function, a minimizer of which provably recovers the latent variables and the graph up to identifiability guarantees. To our knowledge, this is the first such result for interventional CRL with general transformations.
In comparison,
von Kügelgen et al. 2023
also use two hard interventions per node to prove identifiability, albeit without providing a provably correct algorithm. Furthermore, the result in
(
von Kügelgen et al. 2023
)
requires that the estimated latent distribution is faithful to the associated candidate graph for all
h\in\mathcal{H}
. Although a faithfulness assumption does not compromise the identifiability result, it is a strong requirement to verify and poses challenges to devise recovery algorithms. In contrast, we only require observational data, which is generally accessible in practice.

Next, we shed light on the role of observational data. Lemma
24
requires only interventional data for identifying the encoder, whereas Lemma
25
uses observational data. We further tighten this result by showing that for DAG recovery, the observational data becomes unnecessary when we have additive noise models and a weak faithfulness condition holds.

Theorem 27

(No Observational Data)
.

Using interventional data from two
coupled
hard environments for which the pair
(q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})
satisfies interventional discrepancy for all
i\in[n]
, suffices to identify the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
up to componentwise diffeomorphisms. If the latent causal model has additive noise,
p(\mathbf{Z})
is twice differentiable and satisfies the adjacency-faithfulness with respect to
\mathcal{G}
, then the latent DAG
\mathcal{G}
is also identifiable.

Proof sketch:
The recovery of latent variables follow from Lemma
24
. For the recovery of latent DAG, we leverage Lemma
7
(iv) under the additive noise assumption. Specifically, using the score difference between environments
\mathcal{E}^{i}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{j}
, we obtain
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)
for all
i,j\in[n],i\neq j
. Finally, we perform at most
n
conditional independence tests to identify all parent sets
\{{\rm{pa}}(i):i\in[n]\}
. See Appendix
C.1
for the complete proof.

6.3
Complete Identifiability under Uncoupled Interventions

In this setting, we consider the case where the interventional environments corresponding to the same nodes are not specified in pairs. That is, not only is it unknown what node is intervened in an environment, additionally the learner also does not know which two environments intervene on the same node. Hence, additionally, we need to determine the correct
coupling
between the interventional environment sets
\mathcal{E}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
as well. However, this is not a straightforward objective since we cannot readily determine the intervention target in an environment. To address this issue, we solve the problems of finding the correct coupling and optimizing the estimated score variations together. Let
\sigma
denote the permutation that takes
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}=(\tilde{I}^{1},\dots,\tilde{I}^{n})
to
\mathcal{I}=(I^{1},\dots,I^{n})
, i.e.,
\sigma(\tilde{I}^{m})=I^{m}
for all
m\in[n]
. We will modify the objective function in (
84
) to solve for permutation
\pi
as an estimate of
\sigma
, in addition to solving for encoder
h
. To this end, we first modify the score change matrix in (
72
) and define
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi)
as

[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi)]_{i,m}\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\,\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\pi_{m}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_{i}\Big]\ ,

(89)

Unlike the case of coupled interventions, we will also leverage the observational data to identify the encoder. As such, we also define the score change matrices for
\mathcal{E}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
with respect to
\mathcal{E}
with entries

\displaystyle[\mathbf{D}(h)]_{i,m}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_{i}\Big]\ ,

(90)

\displaystyle[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)]_{i,m}

\displaystyle\triangleq{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};h)\big|_{i}\Big]\ .

(91)

Encoder and coupling estimation.

We modify the objective in (
84
) and solve the following constrained optimization problem for any
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
:

\displaystyle\min\limits_{h,\pi}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi)

\displaystyle-\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{\rm F}^{2}\;+\;\lambda\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\|h^{-1}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{X}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]

(92)

\displaystyle\mbox{such that}

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}=\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)\}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\pi}^{\top}

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}\odot\mathds{1}\{[\mathbf{D}(h)]^{\top}\}=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\ .

Note that for the true encoder
g^{-1}
, both
\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})
and
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(g^{-1})
capture the graph structure via Lemma
7
(i), albeit under different permutations. Hence, this equivalence of graph structures for the learned encoder is ensured by the first constraint of
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}=\mathbf{P}_{\pi}\cdot\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)\}
. Also, the acyclicity of the graphs implied by the score differences is ensured by the second constraint, that is
\mathbf{D}(h)
does not contain 2-cycles. To prove that solving this problem leads to the identifiability of the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
, we first show that there exists a global minimizer of this problem.

Lemma 0

(Existence)
.

Encoder
h^{*}=\big[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)\big]^{\top}\cdot g^{-1}
and permutation
\pi^{*}=\sigma
minimize the objective in (
92
).

Proof sketch:
See Appendix
C.2
for the complete proof. The main intuition is that
\pi^{*}=\sigma
makes the problem more similar to the case of coupled interventions. The proof then follows by using the results of the coupled interventions to show that
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)
is a scaled permutation matrix, using (
77
) to derive the relationship between the score differences under
\hat{h}^{*}
and the true encoder
g^{-1}
, and using the sparsity structure of
\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})
to show that the constraints are satisfied.

Next, we show that the objective in (
92
) can attain its minimum value zero
only
for the correct coupling.

Lemma 0

(Feasibility)
.

The optimization problem in (
92
) attains its minimum value zero only for the correct coupling
\pi=\sigma
.

Proof sketch:
See Appendix
C.3
for the complete proof.
The main intuition is that the constraints make it impossible to achieve
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h;\pi)=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
for an incorrect coupling
\pi\neq\sigma
. We prove it by contradiction. We assume that
h^{*}
is a solution, hence,
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*})=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
, and
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}=\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)\}
. Then, by scrutinizing the
eldest
node in
\mathcal{G}
with mismatched interventional environments, we show that
\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\odot[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})]^{\top}
cannot be a diagonal matrix, which contradicts the premise that
h^{*}
is a feasible solution of (
84
).

Finally, we denote the minimizer encoder of (
92
) by
\hat{h}
and form the graph estimate similarly to the coupled intervention case via (
86
). Combining these results, we present our strongest result for general transformations.

Theorem 30

(General – Uncoupled Environments)
.

Using observational data and interventional data from two
uncoupled
hard environments for which each pair in
(p_{i},q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})
satisfies interventional discrepancy for all
i\in[n]
, suffice to identify (i) the latent DAG
\mathcal{G}
perfectly and (ii) the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
up to componentwise diffeomorphisms.

Proof:

Lemma
29
shows that minimizers of the objective in (
92
) necessarily have
\pi=\sigma
. Under this correct coupling, any minimizer of the constrained optimization problem in (
92
) that attains the minimum value zero is also a minimizer of the unconstrained optimization problem in (
84
). Finally, Lemma
24
and Lemma
28
show that such a shared minimizer exists, that is
h^{*}=[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1})]^{\top}\cdot g^{-1}
. Hence, by Theorem
26
, minimizer
\hat{h}
of (
92
) satisfies componentwise latent recovery. Similarly, using
\hat{h}
, proof of the perfect graph recovery follows from Lemma
25
.

\square

Theorem
30
shows that using observational data enables us to resolve any mismatch between the uncoupled environment sets and shows identifiability in the setting of uncoupled environments. This generalizes the identifiability result of
von Kügelgen et al. 2023
, which requires coupled environments.

Comparison to Theorem
26
.

We note that Theorem
30
requires slightly stronger interventional discrepancy conditions than Theorem
26
. In particular, when environments are coupled, we only need
(q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})
to satisfy the interventional discrepancy. On the other hand, to find the correct coupling while performing CRL, Theorem
30
requires each of the pairs
\{(q_{i},\tilde{q}_{i}),(p_{i},q_{i}),(p_{i},\tilde{q}_{i})\}
satisfy interventional discrepancy.

Remark 0
.

For the nonparametric identifiability results, having an oracle that solves the functional optimization problems (
84
) and (
92
) is sufficient. Solving these two problems in their most general form requires calculus of variations. These two problems, however, for any desired parameterized family of functions
\mathcal{H}
(e.g., linear, polynomial, and neural networks), reduce to parametric optimization problems.

6.4
Intervention Extrapolation without CRL

So far, we have studied learning the latent causal representations using the data from single-node interventional environments. A related research problem is extrapolating to unseen combinations of interventions. Namely, given a set of interventions
\mathcal{I}=\{I^{1},\dots,I^{k}\}
, it is desired to emulate the data from an unseen combination of these interventions, e.g., accessing multi-node interventional data using only the single-node interventional data. This is especially important in domains where interventions can be costly or not viable, e.g., not all combinations of different drugs can be clinically tested. Causal representation learning literature has also taken an interest in this problem. Specifically,
Zhang et al. 2023
show for polynomial transformations that given interventions
\mathcal{I}=\{I^{1},\dots,I^{k}\}
, one can sample from any intervention
I\subseteq\mathcal{I}

after
learning the latent representations. We argue that extrapolation to unseen combinations of interventions can be achieved on the observed space
without performing CRL
for the general transformations.

Consider two single-node interventional environments,
\mathcal{E}^{1}
and
\mathcal{E}^{2}
. Without loss of generality, suppose that
I^{1}=\{1\}
and
I^{2}=\{2\}
. Also consider the observational environment
\mathcal{E}^{0}
with
I^{0}=\emptyset
and the
unseen
double-node interventional environment
\mathcal{E}^{m}
with
I^{m}=\{1,2\}
. First, using the score function decompositions in (
18
) and (
19
), we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{1}(z_{1}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(1)})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{2}(z_{2}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(2)})+\sum_{k=3}^{n}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{k}(z_{k}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(k)})\ ,

(93)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{1}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{1}(z_{1}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(1)})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{2}(z_{2}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(2)})+\sum_{k=3}^{n}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{k}(z_{k}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(k)})\ ,

(94)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{2}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{1}(z_{1}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(1)})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{2}(z_{2}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(2)})+\sum_{k=3}^{n}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{k}(z_{k}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(k)})\ ,

(95)

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{1}(z_{1}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(1)})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{2}(z_{2}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(2)})+\sum_{k=3}^{n}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{k}(z_{k}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(k)})\ .

(96)

Then, we have

\displaystyle\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{1}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\big)+\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{2}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\big)={\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\ .

(97)

Next, recall that Lemma
8
gives us the relationship for going from the latent score differences to observed score differences. Applying it to observed
\mathbf{X}
in environment pairs
(\mathcal{E}^{1},\mathcal{E}^{0})
,
(\mathcal{E}^{2},\mathcal{E}^{0})
, and
(\mathcal{E}^{m},\mathcal{E}^{0})
, and using (
97
) we obtain

\displaystyle\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{1}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\big)+\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{2}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\big)

\displaystyle=\Big[[J_{g}(\mathbf{z})]^{\dagger}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\Big[\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{1}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\big)+\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{2}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\big)\Big]

(98)

\displaystyle=\Big[[J_{g}(\mathbf{z})]^{\dagger}\Big]^{\top}\big({\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})\big)

(99)

\displaystyle{=}{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\ .

(100)

This means that, given score functions of observed data from environments
\mathcal{E}^{0}
,
\mathcal{E}^{1}
, and
\mathcal{E}^{2}
, we can obtain the score function of the unseen interventional environment with
I^{m}=\{I^{1},I^{2}\}
, and subsequently generate data from this synthetic environment (using techniques like Langevin sampling
(
Welling and Teh 2011
)
that uses score function as the drift vector field). Note that this process does not require learning the latent causal representations and can be applied to obtain the observed score function
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}
for any intervention
I^{m}\subset\mathcal{I}
.

Finally, we note that concurrent work by
Jain et al. 2024
makes a similar observation for the extrapolation of two single-node interventions without performing CRL. In particular,
(
Jain et al. 2024
)
focuses on detecting pairwise interactions between two biological perturbations (i.e., interventions), investigates the conditions under which two perturbations are
separable
, e.g., distinct single-node interventions, and designs statistical tests for the separability of two perturbations. If the two perturbations are confirmed to be separable, our results show that we can use their score functions to generate synthetic environments that emulate combined perturbation effects.

7
Empirical Evaluations

In this section, we provide empirical assessments of our theoretical guarantees. Specifically, we empirically evaluate the performance of the LSCALE-I (Section
7.1
) and GSCALE-I (Section
7.2
) algorithms for recovering the latent causal variables
\mathbf{Z}
and the latent DAG
\mathcal{G}
on synthetic data. In Section
7.3
, we compare the performance of LSCALE-I to those of the existing algorithms in the closely related literature on both synthetic and biological data.
Next, we also apply GSCALE-I on image data to demonstrate the potential of our approach in realistic high-dimensional datasets (Section
7.5
). Finally, we note that any desired score estimators can be modularly incorporated into our algorithms. In Section
7.6
, we assess our performance’s sensitivity to the estimators’ quality.
7
7

7

The codebase for the algorithms and simulations are available at:

https://github.com/acarturk-e/score-based-crl
.
Additional results and further implementation details are deferred to Appendix
E
.

Evaluation metrics.

The objectives are recovering the graph
\mathcal{G}
and the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
. We use the following metrics to evaluate the accuracy of LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I for recovering these (depending on the specifics of the transformations and interventions, we will have more specific metrics as well). For each metric, we will report the mean and standard error over multiple runs.

•

Structural Hamming distance:
For assessing the recovery of the latent DAG, we report structural Hamming distance (SHD) between the estimate
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and true DAG
\mathcal{G}
. This captures the number of edge operations (add, delete, flip) needed to transform
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
to
\mathcal{G}
.

•

Mean correlation coefficient:
For the recovery of the latent variables, we use mean correlation coefficient (MCC), which was introduced in
(
Khemakhem et al. 2020b
)
and commonly used as a standard metric in CRL. Specifically, MCC measures linear correlations between the estimated and ground truth latent variables. Since the recovery of latent variables is up to permutations, it is reported for the best matching permutation between the components of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\mathbf{Z}
, i.e.,

{\rm MCC}(\mathbf{Z},\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\triangleq\max_{\pi}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n]}{\rm corr}(Z_{i},\hat{Z}_{\pi(i)})\ .

(101)

Score functions.

LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I algorithms, in their first steps, compute estimates of the score differences in the observational environment. The designs of our algorithms are agnostic to how this is performed, i.e., any reliable method for estimating the score differences can be adopted and incorporated into our algorithms in a modular way. In our experiments, we adopt two score estimators necessary for describing the different aspects of our results.

•

Perfect score oracle for identifiability:
Identifiability, by definition, refers to the possibility of recovering the causal graph and latent variables under all idealized assumptions for the data. Assessing the identifiability guarantees formalized in Theorems
14
–
30
requires using
perfect
estimates for the score differences. Hence, we adopt a perfect score oracle for evaluating identifiability. Specifically, we use a perfect score oracle that computes the score difference inputs in LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I by leveraging Lemma
8
and using the ground truth score functions
s,s^{m}
and
\tilde{s}^{m}
(see Appendix
E.1
for details).

•

Data-driven score estimates:
For evaluating the accuracy of our algorithms in practice, we need real score estimates, which are inevitably noisy.
For this purpose, when the pdf
p_{\mathbf{X}}
has a parametric form and score function
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
has a closed-form expression, then we can estimate the parameters to form an estimated score function. For instance, when
\mathbf{X}
follows a linear Gaussian distribution, then we have
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})=-\Theta\cdot\mathbf{x}
in which
\Theta
is the precision matrix of
\mathbf{X}
and can be estimated from samples of
\mathbf{X}
. In other cases in which
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
does not have a known closed-form, we adopt non-parametric score estimators. In particular, we use sliced score matching with variance reduction (SSM-VR) for score estimation due to its efficiency and accuracy for downstream tasks
(
Song et al. 2020
)
. We also introduce a classification-based score difference estimation method, inspired by
Gutmann and Hyvärinen 2012
. The key observation is that given two distributions
p
and
q
, the optimal minimum cross-entropy classifier for distinguishing the samples from two distributions is the log density ratio function
\log p/q
. The difference between the score functions of these distributions,
\nabla\log p-\nabla\log q=\nabla\log p/q
is exactly the gradient of the learned function, which enables us to directly estimate score differences using a classifier.

7.1
LSCALE-I Algorithm for Linear Transformations

Data generation.

To generate
\mathcal{G}
, we use Erdős-Rényi model with density
0.5
and
n\in\{5,8\}
nodes, which is generally the size of the latent graphs considered in CRL literature. We consider the observed dimension
d=100
and generate 100 latent graphs. For the causal mechanisms, we adopt both linear and nonlinear models:

1.

Linear causal model:
We adopt the linear Gaussian model with

Z_{i}=\mathbf{A}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Z}+N_{i}\ ,\qquad\forall i\in[n]\ ,

(102)

where
\mathbf{A}_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{1\times n}
are the rows of the weight matrix
\mathbf{A}
in which
\mathbf{A}_{i,j}\neq 0
if and only
j\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
. The non-zero edge weights are sampled from
{\rm Unif}(\pm[0.5,1.5])
, and the noise terms are zero-mean Gaussian variables with variances
\sigma_{i}^{2}
sampled from
{\rm Unif}([0.5,1.5])
. For node
i
, a hard intervention is given by
Z_{i}=\bar{N}_{i}
where
\bar{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{4})
, and a soft intervention is given by
Z_{i}=\frac{\bar{\mathbf{A}}_{i}}{2}\cdot\mathbf{Z}+N_{i}
.

2.

Quadratic causal model:
We adopt an additive noise model with

\displaystyle Z_{i}=\sqrt{\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{Q}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}}+N_{i}\ ,

(103)

where
\{\mathbf{Q}_{i}:i\in[n]\}
are positive-definite matrices, and the noise terms are zero-mean Gaussian variables with variances
\sigma_{i}^{2}
sampled randomly from
{\rm Unif}([0.5,1.5])
. For node
i
, a hard intervention is given by
Z_{i}=\bar{N}_{i}
where
\bar{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,5\cdot\sigma_{i}^{2})
to ensure a non-negligible change. A soft intervention is given by

Z_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{Q}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}}+\bar{N}_{i}\ .

3.

Multi-layer perceptron (MLP) causal model
: We adopt an additive noise model with
Z_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}
where
f_{i}
is parameterized as a randomly initialized two-layer MLP, with hidden dimension 32 and ReLU activation function. For node
i
, a hard intervention is given by
Z_{i}=\bar{N}_{i}
where
\mathcal{N}(0,5\cdot\sigma_{i}^{2})
, and a soft intervention is given by
Z_{i}=\frac{1}{2}f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+\bar{N}_{i}
.

For each graph, we sample
n_{\rm s}
independent and identically distributed (i.i.d.) samples of
\mathbf{Z}
from each environment. We consider
n_{\rm s}\in\{5000,10000,50000\}
to investigate the effect of the number of samples on the performance of LSCALE-I. The observed variables
\mathbf{X}
are generated according to
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}
, in which
\mathbf{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times n}
is randomly sampled full-rank matrix.

7.1.1
Hard Interventions

Theorem
16
ensures scaling consistency and perfect DAG recovery under hard interventions for linear transformations. As such, we assess the recovery of the latent DAG by the SHD between the estimate
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and true graph
\mathcal{G}
. For latent variable recovery, we report MCC between
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\mathbf{Z}
.
Note that
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=(\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G})\cdot\mathbf{Z}
implies that the effective transform recovery can also be measured by the closeness of
\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}
to the identity matrix. Therefore, in addition to MCC, we also report the normalized
effective transform error
, defined as

\ell_{\rm scale}\triangleq\|\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}-\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\|_{2}\ .

(104)

Table
3
shows the performance of the LSCALE-I algorithm using perfect scores and noisy scores under hard interventions on linear causal models. The first observation is that we have excellent performance at latent variable recovery, demonstrated by perfect MCC and nearly zero effective transform error
\ell_{\rm scale}
, even when using noisy score estimates. For graph recovery, SHD between the estimated and true latent graphs reduces to less than 0.1 even when using noisy scores given enough samples, e.g.,
n_{s}=50000
in Table
3
.
Another key observation is that the results remain consistent while the dimension of observed variables increases from
d=25
to
d=200
, as shown in Table
4
. This confirms our analysis that the performance of LSCALE-I is agnostic to the dimension of the observations. Hence, we suffice by using
d=100
for the rest of the experiments.

Causal models with more complex score functions.

Next, we test the performance of LSCALE-I on quadratic and MLP causal models specified earlier. A difference in this setting compared to linear causal models is that we estimate the score functions of the observed variables using SSM-VR
(
Song et al. 2020
)
as
p_{\mathbf{X}}
is not amenable to parameter estimation.
For quadratic causal models, Table
5
shows that using perfect scores, LSCALE-I performs nearly perfectly. Under noisy scores, an MCC of 0.93 indicates a strong performance for the latent variable recovery. The graph recovery performance suffers more from noisy score estimates, yet it remains reasonable with an approximate SHD of
2.62
(where the expected number of true edges is
5
). Table
6
shows a similar trend, where perfect scores ensure perfect performance, whereas noisy score estimates lead to a degradation in graph recovery performance.

Effect of noisy score estimates on graph recovery under noisy scores.

Table
5
and Table
6
show that graph recovery performance suffers more than the latent variable recovery under noisy score estimates. This discrepancy is due to the difficulty of applying (
59
) under noisy scores. Specifically, we observe that the nonzero entries in true score differences (entries of
{\boldsymbol{d}}^{m}
) can be small, especially for MLP causal models. As such, under noisy score estimates, it becomes more difficult to successfully threshold the empirical score difference quantities to identify the edges of the latent graph. In Section
7.6
, we provide further empirical evaluations on the effect of score estimation quality on the performance of LSCALE-I.

7.1.2
Soft Interventions

To evaluate soft interventions, we first consider linear causal models. In this setting, Theorem
14
ensures the recovery of the latent variables and the latent DAG up to ancestors when using soft interventions. So, we report the SHD between the transitive closure of the estimate
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm tc}
and that of the true graph
\mathcal{G}_{\rm tc}
.
Recall that recovery of latent variables for generic soft interventions is guaranteed up to mixing with parents. To measure the accuracy of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}
with respect to this guarantee, we define
\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa}
with entries
[\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa}]_{i,j}=\mathds{1}(j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i))
, and define
\ell_{\rm pa}
by

\ell_{\rm pa}\triangleq\|\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\odot(\boldsymbol{1}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa})\|_{2}\ ,

(105)

which effectively measures the effect of incorrect mixing in estimated latent variables.
Table
7
shows that by using perfect scores from as few as
n_{\rm s}=5000
samples, we meet the theoretical identifiability guarantees, implied by zero values of SHD and incorrect mixing norm
\ell_{\rm pa}
. Similar to the case of hard interventions, we observe that increasing the number of samples improves the performance under noisy scores. For instance, given
n_{\rm s}=50000
samples, the average SHD between the transitive closures
\hat{\mathcal{G}}_{\rm tc}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\rm tc}
is approximately
0.5
for
n=8
, where graph density being
0.5
implies that the expected number of edges is
14
. Also, the latent variable recovery becomes near perfect, indicated by MCC of
0.98
and near zero incorrect mixing norm
\ell_{\rm pa}
.

Next, we consider quadratic causal models. In this case, Assumption
2
is satisfied, and Theorem
18
ensures the perfect recovery of the latent DAG and the recovery of the latent variables up to surrounding variables. Hence, we report SHD between true
\mathcal{G}
and estimate
\hat{G}
, and the incorrect mixing norm for this setting given by

\ell_{\rm sur}\triangleq\|\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\odot(\boldsymbol{1}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{L}_{\rm sur})\|_{2}\ ,

(106)

where
\mathbf{L}_{\rm sur}
is defined with entries
[\mathbf{L}_{\rm pa}]_{i,j}=\mathds{1}(j\in\overline{{\rm{sur}}}(i))
.
Table
8
shows that when using perfect scores, LSCALE-I performs nearly perfectly, verifying the results of Theorem
18
. Similarly to the case of hard interventions, the performance of LSCALE-I suffers under noisy score estimations while it still remains reasonable, e.g., SHD of
2.79
with respect to the true graph when using only soft interventions.

7.2
GSCALE-I Algorithm for General Transformations

Next, we focus on nonlinear transformations to showcase the settings for which the existing interventional CRL literature lacks provably correct algorithms and provides only identifiability results under a nonlinear transformation.

Choice of nonlinearity.

In this section, we consider M-layer perceptrons with
\tanh
activation as our class of nonlinear transformations. Specifically, we consider functions of the form

\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z})=(\tanh\circ\mathbf{A}^{M}\circ\cdots\tanh\circ\mathbf{A}^{1})(\mathbf{Z})\ ,

(107)

in which
\tanh
is applied elementwise, and parameters
\{\mathbf{A}^{1},\dots,\mathbf{A}^{M}\}
are compatible, randomly sampled
full column rank
matrices. We consider two settings of increasing complexity: 1-layer MLP, and 3-layer MLP (for nonparametric transformations, see experiments on images in Section
7.5
).

7.2.1
Single-layer MLP

First, consider the single-layer perceptron as the nonlinear transformation
g
, given by

\mathbf{X}=g(\mathbf{Z})=\tanh(\bG\cdot\bZ)\ ,

(108)

where
\mathbf{G}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d\times n}
.
Because it is a relatively simple nonlinear transformation, this setting allows us to evaluate the performance of GSCALE-I better. Specifically, leveraging (
108
), we parameterize valid encoders
h
with parameter
\mathbf{H}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times d}
, which gives

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h)

\displaystyle=h(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{H}\cdot\rm arctanh(\mathbf{X})\ ,

(109)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle=h^{-1}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};h))=\tanh(\bH^{\dag} \cdot\hat\bZ(\bX;h))\ ,

(110)

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ .

(111)

This is equivalent to reducing the original non-parametric functional estimation problem to a finite-dimensional parameter estimation problem. This enables stable training and large-scale testing. Furthermore, this parametrization enables us to compute the ground truth score functions of the observed variables, which enables directly assessing the effect of score estimation errors in
GSCALE-I
performance.

Data generation.

In this setting, we focus on quadratic causal models. To generate
\mathcal{G}
we use the Erdős-Rényi model with density
0.5
and
n\in\{5,8\}
nodes. For observational causal mechanisms, we use (
103
) and the parameterization therein. For the two hard interventions on node
i
,
Z_{i}
is set to
N_{q,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{q,i}^{2})
and
N_{\tilde{q},i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2})
, where we set
\sigma_{q,i}^{2}=4\cdot\sigma_{i}^{2}
and
\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2}=9\cdot\sigma_{i}^{2}
. Similarly to LSCALE-I experiments, we consider target dimension
d=100
and we generate 20 latent graphs and
n_{\rm s}=30000
samples per environment for each graph.

Results.

Recall that for general transformations, we can guarantee
\hat{Z}_{i}=\phi_{i}(Z_{i})
for a diffeomorphism
\phi_{i}
. However, MCC
(Z_{i},\phi(Z_{i}))
can largely deviate from 1 for a nonlinear
\phi_{i}
. For instance, for
Z_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
and
\phi(Z_{i})=Z_{i}^{3}+0.1Z_{i}
, we have MCC
(Z_{i},\phi(Z_{i}))\approx 0.786
. On the other hand, when we use the parameterization in (
109
), the only elementwise diffeomorphism between
\mathbf{Z}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
is an elementwise scaling. Therefore, MCC remains a perfectly informative metric. Table
9
shows that we can almost perfectly recover the latent variables (indicated by MCC of
1
) latent DAG for
n=5
nodes by using perfect scores. Furthermore, increasing the latent dimension to
n=8
does not significantly affect performance.

Table 9
:

two coupled hard

n_{\rm score}=30000
samples.

The effect of the quality of score estimation.

When using noisy scores, the performance of GSCALE-I degrades significantly. For instance, MCC goes down to approximately
0.75
for
n=5
and
0.71
for
n=8
. This degradation is similar to what happens when using LSCALE-I on quadratic causal models in Table
8
. It is noteworthy that the transition from perfect to noisy scores is remarkably smoother when using LSCALE-I on linear causal models (Tables
3
and
7
). This discrepancy is attributed to the distinct score estimation procedures adopted in the two experiment settings. Specifically, linear Gaussian latent models allow us to directly estimate the parameters of the closed-form score function
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
. However, when using a quadratic latent model, we rely on a nonparametric score estimation via SSM-VR
(
Song et al. 2020
)
. This comparison between two experiment settings and results underscores that the performance gap between the theoretical guarantees and practical results can be significantly mitigated through the advances in general score estimation techniques. In Section
7.6
, we provide a further empirical evaluation of how the quality of the score estimation affects the final performance.

7.2.2
Multi-layer nonlinearity

Next, we consider general MLPs by setting the number of layers to 3, which is in line with the MLP depths considered in CRL literature
(
Liang et al. 2023
)
. In this setting, we do not use any knowledge of the transformation parametrization and aim to learn a generic NN-based encoder-decoder pair
h,h^{-1}
parameterized by
\theta_{\rm enc},\theta_{\rm dec}
.
In essence, this enables us to test our algorithm’s performance on a standard procedure of function approximation via neural networks.

Data generation.

In this setting, we focus on linear causal models. To generate
\mathcal{G}
, we use the Erdős-Rényi model with density
0.5
and
n=5
nodes. For observational causal mechanisms, we use (
102
) and the parameterization therein. For the two hard interventions on node
i
,
Z_{i}
is set to
N_{q,i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{q,i}^{2})
and
N_{\tilde{q},i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2})
, where we set
\sigma_{q,i}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}/4
and
\sigma_{\tilde{q},i}^{2}=\sigma_{i}^{2}\cdot 4
. Due to the increased computational complexity of training, we consider target dimension value
d=5
, and we generate 5 latent graphs and
n_{\rm s}=10000
samples per graph.

Results.

GSCALE-I algorithm ensures latent variables recovery up to elementwise transformations. As a proxy, we report its linear analogue, i.e., MCC. We summarize the results of these experiments in Table
10
.

Table 10
:
MCC across different runs in MLP transform + MLP causal model setting

Runs

0.64

0.54\pm 0.03

In this setting, we obtain a smaller average MCC rate (
0.54
) compared to the 1-layer nonlinearity experiments in Table
9
. Performance degradation can be attributed to two factors: First, making the transformation more complex and making it more difficult to construct
any
kind of autoencoder. In contrast to the parametric single-layer MLP setting in the previous section, fitting a generic NN has considerably higher sample complexity. Second, our methodology requires the estimation of score functions with relatively good accuracy. However, complex distributions can push forward distributions into very complex and, similarly, difficult-to-estimate landscapes, which can limit the accuracy of our algorithms.

7.3
Comparison with the Existing CRL Studies

In this section, we compare the performance of LSCALE-I with those of the approaches designed for comparable settings in
(
Squires et al. 2023
;
Zhang et al. 2023
)
.

(

)
. As listed in Table
1
and discussed in Section
5
,
(

)
presents identifiability guarantees and provides algorithms for linear transformations with linear causal models. For a fair comparison, we perform comparisons for linear Gaussian SEMs under the parameterization detailed in Section
7.1
. Experiments with synthetic linear causal models in
(
Squires et al. 2023
)
mostly focus on recovering the parameters of the true encoder
\mathbf{G}^{\dagger}
. Hence, in Table
11
, we report performance comparisons in terms of the latent variable recovery metric
\ell_{\rm scale}
defined in (
104
). Table
11
shows that LSCALE-I achieves close to perfect accuracy with as few as
n_{s}=5000
samples, whereas the algorithm of
Squires et al. 2023
requires significantly more samples (more than
n_{s}=50,000
) to achieve a reasonable performance.

In this section, we compare LSCALE-I with the DiscrepancyVAE approach of
(

)
on the setting described in Section
7.1
. We consider a single run of the linear causal model setting with latent dimension
n=8
, observed dimension

, and

3
, we plot the ground truth latent graph (Figure
3
(a)), the estimate generated by LSCALE-I (Figure

.

7.3.3
Comparison with DiscrepancyVAE on biological data

In this section, we apply our algorithms to the Perturb-seq dataset of
Norman et al. 2019
, which is also used in
Zhang et al. 2023
. Following the same pre-processing steps, there are 8,907 unperturbed and 99,590 perturbed cells. Each cell (sample) is denoted by a sparse vector of dimension 5000 that denotes the expressions of select genes. The perturbed cells are generated by CRISPR activation
(
Gilbert et al. 2014
)
on one or two target genes out of 105 genes. Hence, this is modeled by a 5000-dimensional observable variable
\mathbf{X}
and 105-dimensional latent variable
\mathbf{Z}
representing the perturbed genes. In this dataset, the ground truth latent graph and variables are unknown, with the exception of limited gene interactions that have been experimentally verified
(
Norman et al. 2019
)
. For graph estimation, instead of working with a latent dimension of 105,
Zhang et al. 2023
first learns groups of perturbation targets subject to a regularity constraint and then learns a latent graph among these groups. Since the methodology of LSCALE-I does not naturally extend to learning such groups of latent nodes, we use the perturbation groupings reported by
Zhang et al. 2023
as our super-nodes and run LSCALE-I to learn a latent graph over them. We list representative perturbation targets from each super-node in Table
12
.

The most important observation is that LSCALE-I and DiscrepancyVAE both estimate a causal edge from DUSP9 to the MAPK1/ETS2 group, which is a gene activation relation that is demonstrated experimentally
(
Norman et al. 2019
)
. Due to the lack of ground truth for other graph edges, comparing the algorithms for recovering other edges is not informative. Nevertheless, we provide the estimated latent graph from LSCALE-I (Figure
4(a)
) and DiscrepancyVAE (Figure
4(b)
) for completeness.
This verifies that our score-based methodology can recover causal genomics relations.

7.4
Intervention Extrapolation on Biological Data

In Section
6.4
, we have demonstrated that it is possible to sample from unseen combinations of given interventions via only learning the score functions of observed variables. We verify this on the Perturb-seq data discussed in Section
7.3
through the following steps:

1.

Collect observed variables’s data from observational distribution
p_{\mathbf{X}}
, two single-node interventions
p_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}
and
p_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}
, and double-node intervention
p_{\mathbf{x}}^{\{1,2\}}
.

2.

Compute score functions
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
,
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}
, and
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}
.

3.

Perform score function extrapolation for double-node intervention via (
100
):
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\{1,2\}}=\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}+\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}-\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
.

4.

Single-node setting: Generate samples from
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}
and
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}
using Langevin dynamics
(
Welling and Teh 2011
)
, compare them to the original samples from
p_{\mathbf{X}}^{1}
and
p_{\mathbf{X}}^{2}
.

5.

Double-node intervention extrapolation: Generate samples from
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\{1,2\}}
using Langevin dynamics, compare them to the original samples from
p_{\mathbf{X}}^{\{1,2\}}
.

We note that
Zhang et al. 2023
also report experiments on the same dataset for the single-node data generation and double-node intervention extrapolation settings. However, as discussed in Section
6.4
, they perform these tasks using the VAE model for CRL, whereas our approach shows that learning the latent causal representations is unnecessary. Similarly to
Zhang et al. 2023
, we report maximum mean discrepancy (MMD)
(
Gretton et al. 2012
)
to measure the performance quantitatively. In Table
13
, we show that our approach performs at least as well as DiscrepancyVAE on the Perturb-seq dataset. Finally, in Appendix
E.4
, we illustrate the simulated interventional distributions via UMAP clustering, which visually supports the performance implied by Table
13
. These results validate the theoretical analysis that we do not need to learn latent variables to perform intervention extrapolation.

:
MMD evaluation for single-node and double-node intervention extrapolation of our score-based approach and DiscrepancyVAE
(
Zhang et al. 2023
)
on Perturb-seq dataset.

7.5
Experiments on Image Data

In this section, we perform experiments on CRL where the general transformation is image rendering, a highly nonlinear transformation, by applying the GSCALE-I algorithm on synthetic image data.

Image data generation.

For image-based experiments, we follow the setup of the closely related studies in
(
Ahuja et al. 2023
;
Buchholz et al. 2023
)
. Specifically, we consider images of the form in Figure
6
, which are generated as follows. The pairs of latent variables
(Z_{2i-1},Z_{2i})
describe the coordinates of the
i
-th ball’s center in a
64\times 64\times 3
RGB image. We use two and three balls in our experiments, which corresponds to
n\in\{4,6\}
latent variables. We sample the latent graph
\mathcal{G}
from Erdős–Rényi model for
n
nodes and set the expected number of edges to
2n
. Given this graph
\mathcal{G}
, we adopt a
truncated
linear Gaussian latent causal model. The details of the linear model are the same as the Section
7.1
, with the addition of a second set of interventions represented by the causal mechanisms
\tilde{q}_{i}
, by setting
Z_{i}=\tilde{N}_{i}
, where
\tilde{N}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{\sigma_{i}^{2}}{4})
. Latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
are sampled from the distribution defined by this linear model, but samples with any coordinate absolute value greater than
1
are discarded to truncate the distribution in the box
[-1,1]^{n}
. For each latent sample
\mathbf{Z}
, we render an image of the balls centered at the specified coordinates (shifted and scaled to fit entirely within rendered image boundaries) with a radius of 8 pixels and use these as our observed data
\mathbf{X}
. Balls are color-coded, filled with three different colors, and the background is white. For both 2 and 3 balls, we generate 5 graphs and
10000
samples from each graph under each environment.

Figure 5
:
Learning the latent variables from images. Autoencoder-1 is trained to minimize only the reconstruction loss. Autoencoder-2 is trained to minimize the score-based loss in (
83
) while using the reconstruction loss as regularization.

Candidate encoder and training.

For learning the latent variables, we construct a two-step autoencoder, depicted in Figure
5
. Specifically, in step one, we train an autoencoder on the observational image data set with a bottleneck dimension of 64 using only a reconstruction objective. In step two, we train another autoencoder on the 64-dimensional output of the first encoder with the bottleneck dimension of
n
(the latent dimension) using both reconstruction loss and the score-based loss described earlier in Section
7.2
. Architecture details and training schedule are given in Table
18
.

Score difference estimation.

The main loss function for the second autoencoder of our setup is
\norm{\bD_{\rm t}(h)-\bI_{n \times n}}_{1,1}
, which is equivalent to the score-based loss in (
83
). For computing
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)
for an encoder
h
, we need to compute the score differences of image datasets. To this end, we adopt a binary classifier-based log density ratio (LDR) estimator and use the gradients of these learned LDRs as our score difference functions, as described earlier. Specifically, we parameterize the LDR at any image through a CNN-based model, details of which are given in Table
19
. The output of this model is used to compute class probabilities, which is used to minimize cross entropy to train the model. We train one LDR model for each pair of hard interventions on the same node and one for the observational-interventional environment pairs for one set of hard interventions.

Figure 6
:
Sample images (top row) versus their reconstructions (bottom row).

Results.

GSCALE-I ensures perfect graph recovery, and latent variables recovery up to permutation and elementwise scaling. Similarly to related work
(
Ahuja et al. 2023
;
Buchholz et al. 2023
)
, we report MCC between
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
and
\mathbf{Z}
as a metric of latent variables recovery. We report the MCC values we observe on 5 runs with 2 and 3 balls in Table
14
.

Table 14
:
