During training for 3 balls, we observed that MCC highly correlates with the reconstruction performance, i.e., when reconstruction errors in both autoencoders converge to small values, latent variable recovery is also successful (e.g., MCC is 0.98 in run-1 for 3 balls). We note that the reconstruction does not have to be perfect. For instance, the samples in Figure
6
are from the setting with an MCC of 0.98. Conversely, for the runs with nonconvergent reconstruction loss in either of the autoencoder training steps, latent variable recovery also struggled (e.g., MCC of 0.49 in run-2). Hence, as long as we can ensure reasonable reconstruction performance when we are minimizing the score-based loss, our algorithm manages to disentangle the true causal variables embedded in the latent domain.

For the setting of 2 balls, our main architecture (with multi-level autoencoder) struggled to converge on the
n=4
bottleneck. Subsequently, we switched to a CNN-based autoencoder to build the first level of our autoencoder. Under this architecture, we obtain an MCC of approximately 0.5.
We report the results from related work in Table
15
and discuss them as follows.

•

Ahuja et al. 2023
considers only two balls. They report MCC for linear SCMs for this setting when using varying numbers of
do
interventions per node. For instance, for 3 interventions per node, they achieve an MCC of 0.73 and require at least 5 interventions per node to achieve a higher MCC (e.g., 0.85). Therefore, while our method struggles at lower dimensional latent variables (
n=4
for two balls), our result on three balls with 0.76 average MCC is highly competitive, considering that we use less restrictive stochastic interventions and two interventions per node.

•

Buchholz et al. 2023
similarly report 5 runs for varying numbers of balls. They report significantly better results than
(
Ahuja et al. 2023
)
via using contrastive learning, e.g., MCC of 0.87 for two balls and 0.94 for five balls. They also report that some other commonly used methods for CRL, e.g., vanilla variational autoencoder, are not competitive in this setting.

Gap between the theory and practice in CRL.

We highlight that the nature of the results presented in this paper is theoretical. We have established unknown identifiability guarantees in several settings. Furthermore, we have designed algorithms by defining a differentiable loss function whose global optima achieves identifiability for general CRL and established that the algorithms generate provably correct representations. The experiments provided on various synthesized and real datasets showcase the potential of the score-based framework on complex non-linear transforms, e.g., image rendering, and we achieve this goal by demonstrating a reasonable performance at latent variable recovery. That being said, the considered image dataset and other synthetic datasets are still simple compared to real-world problems, and there remains a gap between the theoretical guarantees and practical applications. In other words, the theory developed is only a
necessary
step for guaranteed practical performance. From our perspective, closing this gap and obtaining
sufficient
conditions for practical performance requires improvements in two aspects. First, using better score difference estimators (which we empirically investigate in Section
7.6
) would enable better application of our score-based CRL framework. Second, designing more suitable architectures for leveraging the invariance properties of causal models can help scale up CRL applications.

7.6
Sensitivity to Score Estimation Noise

As discussed earlier, score estimators can be modularly incorporated into our algorithm.
For the cases where
p_{\mathbf{X}}
is not amenable to parameter estimation, we can use the noisy score estimates generated by SSM-VR as achievable baselines as presented through this section. In this subsection, we assess how much performance improvement we can have as the score estimates become more accurate. To this end, we test the LSCALE-I algorithm under varying score estimation noise levels. Specifically, we run the LSCALE-I algorithm using the scores generated by the following model:

\hat{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x};\sigma^{2})=\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})\cdot\big(1+\Xi\big)\ ,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\Xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}\cdot\mathbf{I}_{d\times d})\ .

(112)

We consider hard interventions on graph size of
n=5
, set
d=25
, and vary the value of
\sigma^{2}
within
[10^{-4},10^{-2}]
. We repeat the experiments 100 times with
n_{\rm s}=10000
samples from each environment. We plot mean normalized
\mathbf{Z}
error, defined as

\ell_{\rm norm}(\mathbf{Z},\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\triangleq\frac{\|\mathbf{Z}-\hat{\mathbf{Z}}\|_{2}}{\norm{\bZ}_{2}}\ .

(113)

and mean SHD in Figure
7
with respect to the signal-to-noise ratio (SNR). It is clear that when the score estimation error is small, indicated by a high SNR value, the LSCALE-I algorithm demonstrates a strong performance in recovering both latent causal variables and the latent graph. We also note that the baseline results with noisy scores computed via SSM-VR in Table
5
yield similar success at graph recovery at the SNR of approximately
25
dB and latent recovery at the SRM of approximately
8
dB. The curves also confirm our observations in Section
7.1
and Section
7.2
that graph recovery is more sensitive to score estimation errors.
The trend of the curves in Figure
7
indicates that our algorithm would greatly benefit from a better score estimator.

(a)

Normalized
\ell_{2}
loss versus SNR

(b)

SHD versus SNR

Figure 7
:
The performance of LSCALE-I under noisy scores with varying SNR for
n=5
and
d=25
. The dashed vertical lines correspond to SNR values that correspond to the performance attained by SSM-VR.

8
Discussion and Concluding Remarks

In this paper, we have proposed a score function-based CRL framework that uses stochastic interventions to learn latent causal representations and the latent causal graph underlying them. In this framework, by uncovering novel connections between score functions and CRL, we have established identifiability results for linear and general transformations without restricting the latent causal models, and designed LSCALE-I and GSCALE-I algorithms that achieve these identifiability guarantees. There are several exciting directions for future work.

As discussed in Section
3.4
after defining the identifiability objectives, and emphasized when presenting the corresponding results through the paper, our results are tight
given a complete set of atomic interventions.
A natural direction for future work is relaxing the atomic intervention requirement. In this aspect, the paper
(
Varıcı et al. 2024
)
extends the score-based framework for linear transformations into the setting of unknown multi-node interventions by using combinations of multi-node score differences. Given a sufficiently diverse multi-node intervention set, similar identifiability results to those in Section
5
are shown. That being said, establishing the
necessary
conditions for multi-node interventions and the case of general transformations remains an open problem.
Next, we note that our LSCALE-I algorithm for
linear transformations
is agnostic to the intervention type and latent causal model, meaning that it can be used for both soft and hard interventions and different causal models. Designing similar universal CRL algorithms that can handle general transformations under different sizes and types of interventional environments is the ultimate goal for studying the identifiability of CRL from interventions. A missing component of existing CRL literature is the finite-sample analysis. Probabilistic identifiability results for a given number of interventional data samples can be useful, especially in applications where performing interventions is costly. In this direction, the recent paper
(
Acartürk et al. 2024
)
establishes finite sample guarantees for CRL under linear transformations. Extending such analysis to general transformations can provide insights for bridging the gap between theory and practice in CRL.

Appendix A
Proofs of Score Function Properties and Transformations

We start by providing the following facts that will be used repeatedly in the proofs.

Proposition 0
.

Consider two continuous functions
f,g:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
with full support. Then, for any
\alpha>0
,

\exists\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;f(\mathbf{z})\neq g(\mathbf{z})\quad\iff\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\big|^{\alpha}\Big]\neq 0\ .

(114)

Specifically, for
\alpha=1
, we have

\exists\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;f(\mathbf{z})\neq g(\mathbf{z})\quad\iff\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})\big|\Big]\neq 0\ .

(115)

Proof:
If there exists
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
f(\mathbf{z})\neq g(\mathbf{z})
, then
f(\mathbf{z})-g(\mathbf{z})
is non-zero over a non-zero-measure set due to continuity. Then,
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})|^{\alpha}\big]\neq 0
since
p
(pdf of
\mathbf{Z}
) has full support. On the other direction, if
f(\mathbf{z})=g(\mathbf{z})
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, then
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})|^{\alpha}\big]=0
. This means that
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})-g(\mathbf{Z})|^{\alpha}\big]\neq 0
implies that there exists
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
f(\mathbf{z})\neq g(\mathbf{z})
.

A.1
Proof of Lemma
7

Our score-based methodology builds on the changes in score functions under interventions. For proving Lemma
7
, we start by showing that
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\implies i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})
, which holds true regardless of the causal model and the intervention type.

Proof of
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\implies i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})
:

Let
\ell
denote the node intervened in
\mathcal{E}^{m}
, i.e.,
I^{m}=\ell
. Recalling (
18
) and (
19
), the latent scores
{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})
and
{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})
are decomposed as

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

(116)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ .

(117)

Hence,
{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})
and
{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})
differ in only the causal mechanism of node
\ell
. Next, we check the derivatives of
p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})
and
q_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})
in their
i
-th coordinates. Note that these two depend on
\mathbf{Z}
only through
\{Z_{j}:\;j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell)\}
. Therefore, if
i\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell)
,

\displaystyle\partialderivative{z_i}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})=\partialderivative{z_i}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})=0\ ,

(118)

which indicates that if
i\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell)
, then
[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}=[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}
for all
\mathbf{z}
. This, equivalently, means that if
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0
, then
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}({\ell})
.
\square

For the reverse direction, we will use the following intermediate result which formalizes the weakest possible requirement for a meaningful intervention and shows that it is a property of (i) hard interventions under any causal model, and (ii) additive noise model under either soft or hard interventions.

Lemma 0

(Interventional Regularity)
.

Causal mechanisms
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
of node
i
are said to satisfy
interventional regularity
if

\displaystyle\exists\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;\mbox{such that}\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\frac{q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\neq 0\ ,\qquad\forall k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)\ .

(119)

Then,
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
satisfy interventional regularity if at least one of the following conditions is true:

1.

The intervention is hard, i.e.,
q_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{i}(z_{i})
.

2.

The causal model is an additive noise model in which the pdfs of the noise variables are analytic.

Proof:
See Appendix
A.3
.

Case (i) and Case (ii).

{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\implies i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})
is already shown above. For the reverse direction, we give the proof for soft interventions on additive noise models, Case (ii). We will use interventional regularity since Lemma
33
shows that it is satisfied for additive noise models. The proof for hard interventions, Case (i), follows from similar arguments since interventional regularity is also satisfied for hard interventions by Lemma
33
.

Proof of
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})\implies{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0
:

Note that the two score functions
s
and
s^{m}
are equal in their coordinate
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell)
only if

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{i}}-\frac{\partial\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{i}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\log\frac{q_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}\ .

(120)

However, (
120
) contradicts with interventional regularity. Therefore, if
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell)
,
[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})_{i}]
and
[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}
are not identical and by Proposition
32
,
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0
.

Case (iii) Coupled environments.

Suppose that
I^{m}=\tilde{I}^{m}=\ell
. Following (
16
), we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

(121)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{\ell})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ .

(122)

Then, subtracting (
122
) from (
121
) and looking at
i
-th coordinate, we have

\displaystyle\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})}{\partial z_{i}}-\dfrac{\partial\log\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{\ell})}{\partial z_{i}}\ .

(123)

If
i\neq\ell
, the right-hand side is zero and we have
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}=0
for all
\mathbf{z}
. On the other hand, if
i=\ell
, since
q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
and
\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
are distinct, there exists
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
such that
q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})\neq\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
. Subsequently, by Proposition
32
, we have
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0
.

Case (iv) Uncoupled environments.

Suppose that
I^{m}=\ell
and
\tilde{I}^{m}=j
, and
\ell\neq j
. Following (
16
), we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{j}(z_{j}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})+\sum_{k\in[n]\setminus\{\ell,j\}}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{k}(z_{k}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(k})\ ,

(124)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{j}(z_{j})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})+\sum_{k\in[n]\setminus\{\ell,j\}}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{k}(z_{k}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(k)})\ .

(125)

Then, subtracting (
125
) from (
124
) we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\;\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})+\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{j}(z_{j}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})-\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{j}(z_{j})-\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})\ .

(126)

Scrutinizing the
i
-th coordinate, we have

\displaystyle\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})}{\partial z_{i}}+\dfrac{\partial\log p_{j}(z_{j}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})}{\partial z_{i}}-\dfrac{\partial\log q_{j}(z_{j})}{\partial z_{i}}-\dfrac{\partial\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{i}}\ .

(127)

Proof of
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\ \implies i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell,j)
:

Suppose that
i\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}({\ell},j)
. Then, none of the terms in the RHS of (
127
) is a function of
z_{i}
. Therefore, all the terms in the RHS of (
127
) are zero, and we have
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}=0
for all
\mathbf{z}
. By Proposition
32
,
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]=0
. This, equivalently, means that if
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0
, then
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}({\ell},j)
.

Proof of
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{i}\Big]\neq 0\impliedby i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\ell,j)
:

We prove it by contradiction. Assume that
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}=0
for all
\mathbf{z}
. Without loss of generality, let
\ell\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)
.

1.

If
i=\ell
.
In this case, (
127
) is simplified to

\displaystyle 0=\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell}=\dfrac{\partial\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})}{\partial z_{\ell}}-\dfrac{\partial\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{\ell}}\ .

(128)

If
\ell
is a root node, i.e.,
{\rm{pa}}(\ell)=\emptyset
, (
128
) implies that
(\log q_{\ell})^{\prime}(z_{\ell})=(\log p_{\ell})^{\prime}(z_{\ell})
for all
z_{\ell}
. Integrating, we get
p_{\ell}(z_{\ell})=\alpha q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
for some constant
\alpha
. Since both
p_{\ell}
and
q_{\ell}
are pdfs, they both integrate to one, implying
\alpha=1
and
p_{\ell}(z_{\ell})=q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
, which contradicts the premise that observational and interventional mechanisms are distinct. If
\ell
is not a root node, consider some
k\in{\rm{pa}}(\ell)
. Then, taking the derivative of (
128
) with respect to
z_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle 0=\dfrac{\partial^{2}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{\ell}\partial z_{k}}\ .

(129)

Recall the equation
Z_{\ell}=f_{\ell}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})+N_{\ell}
for additive noise models specified in (
7
). Denote the pdf of the noise term
N_{\ell}
by
p_{N}
. Then, the conditional pdf
p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})
is given by
p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})=p_{N}(z_{\ell}-f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}))
. Denoting the score function of
p_{N}
by
r_{p}
,

\displaystyle r_{p}(u)\triangleq\dfrac{\rm d}{{\rm d}u}\log p_{N}(u)\ ,

(130)

we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{\partial\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{\ell}}=\dfrac{\partial\log p_{N}(z_{\ell}-f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}))}{\partial z_{\ell}}=r_{p}(z_{\ell}-f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}))\ .

(131)

Substituting this into (
129
), we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\dfrac{\partial r_{p}\big(z_{\ell}-f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})\big)}{\partial z_{k}}=-\dfrac{\partial f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot r_{p}^{\prime}\big(z_{\ell}-f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})\big)\ ,\quad\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ .

(132)

Since
k
is a parent of
\ell
, there exists a fixed
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}=\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}^{*}
realization for which
\partial f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}^{*})/\partial z_{k}
is non-zero. Otherwise,
f_{\ell}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})
would not be sensitive to
z_{k}
which is contradictory to
k
being a parent of
\ell
. Note that
Z_{\ell}
can vary freely after fixing
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)}
. Therefore, for (
132
) to hold, the derivative of
r_{p}
must always be zero. However, the score function of a valid pdf with full support cannot be constant. Therefore,
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})_{i}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}
is not always zero, and we have
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0
.

2.

If
i\neq\ell
.
In this case, (
127
) is simplified to

\displaystyle 0=\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}=\dfrac{\partial\log p_{j}(z_{j}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})}{\partial z_{i}}-\dfrac{\partial\log q_{j}(z_{j})}{\partial z_{i}}-\dfrac{\partial\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{i}}\ .

(133)

We investigate case by case and reach a contradiction for each case. First, suppose that
i\notin{\rm{pa}}({\ell})
. Then, we have
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)
, and (
133
) becomes

\displaystyle 0=\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}=\dfrac{\partial\log p_{j}(z_{j}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})}{\partial z_{i}}-\dfrac{\partial\log q_{j}(z_{j})}{\partial z_{i}}\ .

(134)

If
i=j
, the impossibility of (
134
) directly follows from the impossibility of (
128
). The remaining case is
i\in{\rm{pa}}(j)
. In this case, taking the derivative of the right-hand side of (
134
) with respect to
z_{j}
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0=\dfrac{\partial^{2}\log p_{j}(z_{j}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{j}}\ ,

(135)

which is a realization of (
129
) for
i\in{\rm{pa}}(j)
and
j
in place of
k\in{\rm{pa}}(\ell)
and
\ell
, which we proved to be impossible in
i=\ell
case. Therefore,
i\notin{\rm{pa}}(\ell)
is not viable. Finally, suppose that
i\in{\rm{pa}}({\ell})
. Then, taking the derivative of the right-hand side of (
133
) with respect to
z_{\ell}
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0=\dfrac{\partial^{2}\log p_{\ell}(z_{\ell}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\ell)})}{\partial z_{i}\partial z_{\ell}}\ ,

(136)

which is again a realization of (
129
) for
k=i
, which we proved to be impossible.

Hence, we showed that
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{i}
cannot be zero for all
\mathbf{z}
values. Then, by Proposition
32
we have
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})|_{i}\big]\neq 0
, and the proof is concluded.

A.2
Proof of Lemma
8

Let us recall the setting. Consider random vectors
\mathbf{Y}_{1},\mathbf{Y}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
and
\mathbf{W}_{1}
,
\mathbf{W}_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
that are related through
\mathbf{Y}_{1}=f(\mathbf{W}_{1})
and
\mathbf{Y}_{2}=f(\mathbf{W}_{2})
such that
r\geq s
, probability measures of
\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{W}_{2}
are absolutely continuous with respect to the
s
-dimensional Lebesgue measure and
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{s}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
is an injective and continuously differentiable function.

In this setting, the realizations of
\mathbf{W}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{1}
, and that of
\mathbf{W}_{2}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{2}
, are related through
\mathbf{y}=f(\mathbf{w})
. Since
f
is injective and continuously differentiable, volume element
{\rm d}\mathbf{w}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
gets mapped to
\left|\det([J_{f}(\bw)]^{\top} \cdot J_{f}(\bw))\right|^{1/2}\ {\rm d}\mathbf{w}
on
{\rm im}(f)
. Since
\mathbf{W}_{1}
has density
p_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}
absolutely continuous with respect to the
s
-dimensional Lebesgue measure, using the area formula
(
Boothby 2003
)
, we can define a density for
\mathbf{Y}_{1}
, denoted by
p_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}
, supported only on manifold
\mathcal{M}\triangleq{\rm im}(f)
which is absolutely continuous with respect to the
s
-dimensional Hausdorff measure:

p_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})=p_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})\cdot\left|\det([J_{f}(\bw)]^{\top} \cdot J_{f}(\bw))\right|^{-1/2}\ ,\quad\mbox{where}\quad\mathbf{y}=f(\mathbf{w})\ .

(137)

Densities
p_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}
and
p_{\mathbf{W}_{2}}
of
\mathbf{Y}_{2}
and
\mathbf{W}_{2}
are related similarly. Subsequently, score functions of
\{\mathbf{W}_{1},\mathbf{W}_{2}\}
and
\{\mathbf{Y}_{1},\mathbf{Y}_{2}\}
are specified similarly to (
9
) and (
14
), respectively. Denote the Jacobian matrix of
f
at point
\mathbf{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
by
J_{f}(\mathbf{w})
, which is an
r\times s
matrix with entries given by

\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]_{i,j}=\partialderivative{\big[f(\bw)\big]_i}{w_j}=\partialderivative{y_i}{w_j}\ ,\quad\forall i\in[r]\,,j\in[s]\ .

(138)

Next, consider a function
\phi\colon\mathcal{M}\to{\mathbb{R}}
. Since the domain of
\phi
is a manifold, its differential, denoted by
D\phi
, is defined according to (
12
). By noting
\mathbf{y}=f(\mathbf{w})
, we can also differentiate
\phi
with respect to
\mathbf{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{s}
as
(
Simon 2014
, p. 57)

\nabla_{w}\phi(\mathbf{y})=\nabla_{w}(\phi\circ f)(\mathbf{w})=\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot D\phi(\mathbf{y})\ .

(139)

Next, given the identities in (
137
) and (
139
), we find the relationship between score functions of
\mathbf{W}_{1}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{1}
as follows.

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\log p_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})

(140)

\displaystyle{=}\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\log p_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})+\nabla_{w}\log\left|\det([J_{f}(\bw)]^{\top} \cdot J_{f}(\bw))\right|^{1/2}

(141)

\displaystyle{=}\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot D\log p_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})+\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\log\left|\det([J_{f}(\bw)]^{\top} \cdot J_{f}(\bw))\right|^{1/2}

(142)

\displaystyle=\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})+\nabla_{w}\log\left|\det([J_{f}(\bw)]^{\top} \cdot J_{f}(\bw))\right|^{1/2}\ .

(143)

Following the similar steps that led to (
143
) for
\mathbf{W}_{2}
and
\mathbf{Y}_{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}_{2}}(\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})+\nabla_{\mathbf{w}}\log\left|\det([J_{f}(\bw)]^{\top} \cdot J_{f}(\bw))\right|^{1/2}\ .

(144)

Subtracting (
144
) from (
143
), we obtain the desired result

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}_{2}}(\mathbf{w})=\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})\big]\ .

(145)

Proof of the reverse direction.

Multiplying (
145
) from left with
\big[[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})]^{{\dagger}}\big]^{\top}
, we obtain

\Big[\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}_{1}}(\mathbf{w})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}_{2}}(\mathbf{w})\big]=\Big[\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})\big]\ .

(146)

Note that

\Big[\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{\top}=J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\cdot\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{{\dagger}}\ .

(147)

By properties of the Moore-Penrose inverse, for any matrix
\mathbf{A}
, we have
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{{\dagger}}\cdot\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{A}
. This means that
\mathbf{A}\cdot\mathbf{A}^{{\dagger}}
acts as a left identity for vectors in the column space of
\mathbf{A}
. By definition,
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}
and
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}
have values in
T_{\mathbf{w}}{\rm im}(f)
, the tangent space of the image manifold
f
at point
\mathbf{w}
. This space is equal to the column space of matrix
J_{f}(\mathbf{w})
. Therefore,
J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\cdot[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})]^{{\dagger}}
acts as a left identity for
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})
and
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})
, and we have

J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\cdot\big[J_{f}(\mathbf{w})\big]^{{\dagger}}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})\big]={\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{1}}(\mathbf{y})-{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}_{2}}(\mathbf{y})\ .

(148)

Substituting (
147
) and (
148
) into (
146
) completes the proof.

Proof of Corollary
9

For a given linear transform
\mathbf{F}
, we have
J_{f}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{F}
, which is independent of
\mathbf{w}
. Then, in the proof of Lemma
8
, (
143
) reduces to
{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{W}}(\mathbf{w})=\mathbf{F}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})
. Finally, we note that the score difference of
\mathbf{Y}
can be similarly written in terms of the score difference of
\mathbf{W}
.

A.3
Proof of Lemma
33

We will prove that causal mechanisms
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
satisfy interventional regularity for (i) hard interventions and (ii) additive noise models. To this end, we first define

\displaystyle\psi(z_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\triangleq\frac{q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\ .

(149)

We start by showing that
\psi(z_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
varies with
z_{i}
. We prove it by contradiction. Assume the contrary, i.e., let
\psi(z_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=\psi(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
. By rearranging (
149
) we have

\displaystyle q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=\psi(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\,p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ .

(150)

Fix a realization of
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*}
and integrate both sides of (
150
) with respect to
z_{i}
. Since both
p_{i}
and
q_{i}
are pdfs, we have

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})\,\differential z_{i}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}\psi(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})\,p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})\mathrm{d}{z_{i}}\differential z_{i}

(151)

\displaystyle=\psi(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})\differential z_{i}

(152)

\displaystyle=\psi(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})\ .

(153)

This identity implies that
p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})=q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*})
for any arbitrary realization
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{*}
. This contradicts the premise that observational and interventional distributions are distinct. As a result, to check if a model satisfies interventional regularity for node
i
, it suffices to investigate whether the function
\psi
is not invariant with respect to
z_{k}
for
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
. To this end, from (
149
) we know that
\psi(z_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
varies with
z_{k}
if and only if

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{i}}\log\psi(z_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=\frac{\partial q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}-\frac{\partial p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\neq 0\ .

(154)

Next, we investigate the sufficient conditions listed in the Lemma
33
.

A.3.1
Hard Interventions

Under hard interventions, note that for any
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\neq 0\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}q_{i}(z_{i})=0\ .

(155)

Then, it follows directly from (
154
) that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\log\psi(z_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})

\displaystyle=\underset{=\,0}{\underbrace{\frac{\partial q_{i}(z_{i})}{\partial z_{k}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{q_{i}(z_{i})}-\underset{\neq\,0}{\underbrace{\frac{\partial p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}}}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\neq 0\ .

(156)

Thus, hard interventions on any latent causal model satisfy interventional regularity.

A.3.2
Additive Noise Models

The additive noise model for node
i
is given by

Z_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}\ ,

(157)

as specified in (
7
). When node
i
is soft intervened,
Z_{i}
is generated according to

Z_{i}=\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+\bar{N}_{i}\ ,

(158)

in which
\bar{f}_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
specify the interventional mechanism for node
i
. Then, denoting the pdfs of
N_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
by
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
, respectively, (
157
) and (
158
) imply that

\displaystyle p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=p_{N}\big(z_{i}-f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{N}\big(z_{i}-\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ .

(159)

Denote the score functions associated with
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
by

\displaystyle r_{p}(u)\triangleq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log p_{N}(u)=\frac{p_{N}^{\prime}(u)}{p_{N}(u)}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad r_{q}(u)\triangleq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log q_{N}(u)=\frac{q_{N}^{\prime}(u)}{q_{N}(u)}\ .

(160)

We will prove that (
154
) holds by contradiction. Assume the contrary and let

\displaystyle\frac{\partial q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}=\frac{\partial p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}\ .

(161)

From (
159
) and (
160
), for the numerators in (
161
) we have,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot p_{N}^{\prime}\big(z_{i}-f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ ,

(162)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\qquad\frac{\partial q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{\partial\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot q_{N}^{\prime}\big(z_{i}-\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ .

(163)

Hence, the identity in (
161
) can be written as

\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot r_{p}\big(z_{i}-f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)=\frac{\partial\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot r_{q}\big(z_{i}-\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ .

(164)

Define
n_{i}
and
\bar{n}_{i}
as the realizations of
N_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
when
Z_{i}=z_{i}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}=\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
. By defining
\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\triangleq f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})-\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
, we have
\bar{n}_{i}=n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
. Then, (
164
) can rewritten as

\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot r_{p}(n_{i})=\frac{\partial\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\cdot r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))\ .

(165)

Note that
\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}
is a non-zero continuous function. Hence, there exists an interval
\Phi\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
over which
\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}\neq 0
. Likewise,
r_{p}(n_{i})
cannot be constantly zero over all possible intervals
\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
. This is because otherwise, it would have to necessarily be a constant zero function (since it is analytic), which is an invalid score function. Hence, there exists an open interval
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}
over which
r_{p}(n_{i})
is non-zero for all
n_{i}\in\Omega
. Then, we can rearrange (
165
) as

\displaystyle\frac{r_{p}(n_{i})}{r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{\partial\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}}{\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}}\ ,\quad\forall(n_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\in\Omega\times\Phi\ .

(166)

Note that the RHS of (
166
) is not a function of
n_{i}
. Then, taking the derivative of both sides with respect to
n_{i}
, we get

\frac{r_{p}^{\prime}(n_{i})}{r_{p}(n_{i})}=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}{r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}\ .

(167)

In the next step, we show that
\delta
is not a constant function. We prove this by contradiction. Suppose that
\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=\delta^{*}
is a constant function. Then, the gradients of
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
are equal. From (
166
), this implies that

r_{p}(n_{i})=r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})\ ,\quad\forall n_{i}\in\Omega\ .

(168)

Since
r_{p}(n_{i})
and
r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
are analytic functions that agree on an open interval of
{\mathbb{R}}
, they are equal for all
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This implies that
p_{N}(n_{i})=\eta q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
for some constant
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
are pdfs,
\eta=1
is the only choice that maintains
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
are pdfs. Therefore,
p_{N}(n_{i})=q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
. However, using (
159
), this implies that
p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
, which contradicts the premise that an intervention changes the causal mechanism of target node
i
. Therefore
\delta
is a continuous, non-constant function, and its image over
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in\Phi
includes an open interval
\Theta\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}
. With this result in mind, we return to (
167
). Consider a fixed realization
n_{i}=n_{i}^{*}
and denote the value of the left-hand side (LHS) for
n_{i}^{*}
by
\alpha
. By defining
u\triangleq\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
, we get

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}{r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}\ ,\qquad\forall u\in\Theta\ .

(169)

This is only possible if
r_{q}
is an exponential function, i.e.,
r_{q}(u)=k_{1}\exp(\alpha u)
over interval
u\in\Theta
. Since
r_{q}
is an analytic function, it is, therefore, exponential over entire
{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, the associated pdf must have the form
q_{N}(u)=k_{2}\exp((k_1/\alpha) \exp(\alpha u))
. However, the integral of this function over the entire domain
{\mathbb{R}}
diverges. Hence, it is not a valid pdf, rendering a contradiction. Hence, the additive noise model satisfies interventional regularity.

Appendix B
Proofs of the Results for Linear Transformations

B.1
Auxiliary Results

First, we provide a linear algebraic property, which will be used in the proofs.

Lemma 0
.

Consider the latent causal graph
\mathcal{G}
, and a matrix
\mathbf{L}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
.

1.

Let
\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}
and
\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}
be binary matrices that denote the parental and ancestral relationships in
\mathcal{G}
, respectively, i.e.,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}]_{i,j}\triangleq\begin{cases}1\ ,&\textnormal{if}\quad j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\\
0\ ,&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}\ ,\;\;\mbox{and}\quad[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}]_{i,j}\triangleq\begin{cases}1\ ,&\textnormal{if}\quad j\in\overline{{\rm{an}}}(i)\\
0\ ,&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}\ .

(170)

Then, if
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}
, we also have
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L}^{-1})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}
.

2.

Let
\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}
be a binary matrix that denotes the surrounding relationships in
\mathcal{G}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,j}

\displaystyle\triangleq\begin{cases}1\ ,&\textnormal{if}\quad\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\subseteq\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(j)\ ,\\
0\ ,&\textnormal{otherwise}\end{cases}\ .

(171)

Then, if
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}
, we also have
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L}^{-1})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}
.

Proof:
First, note that
\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}
. Hence, we start with considering a generic lower triangular matrix
\mathbf{L}
such that
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}
. Matrix
\mathbf{L}
can be decomposed as

\mathbf{L}=\mathbf{E}\cdot(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})\ ,

(172)

in which
\mathbf{E}
is a diagonal matrix and
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is a strictly lower triangular matrix that satisfies
\mathds{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}
. Subsequently, since
\mathbf{E}
is a diagonal matrix, we have

\mathbf{L}^{-1}=(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{E}^{-1}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{L}^{-1}\}=\mathds{1}\big\{(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}\big\}\ .

(173)

Note that
\mathbf{\Lambda}
is a strictly lower triangular
n\times n
matrix, which implies that
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{n}
is a zero matrix. Therefore, the inverse of
(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})
can be expanded as

(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{\Lambda}+\mathbf{\Lambda}^{2}-\dots+(-1)^{n-1}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{n-1}\ .

(174)

If
\big[\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda}\big]^{-1}_{i,j}\neq 0
for some
i,j\in[n]
with
j<i
, by (
174
) we have
[\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k}]_{i,j}\neq 0
for some
k\in[n-1]
. Expanding matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k}
yields that
[\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k}]_{i,j}
is equal to the sum of the products with
k
terms, i.e.,

[\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k}]_{i,j}=\sum_{i>a_{1}>\dots>a_{k-1}>j}[\mathbf{\Lambda}]_{i,a_{1}}[\mathbf{\Lambda}]_{a_{1},a_{2}}\dots[\mathbf{\Lambda}]_{a_{k-1},j}\ .

(175)

Therefore, if
[\mathbf{\Lambda}^{k}]_{i,j}\neq 0
, there exists a sequence of entries
\big([\mathbf{\Lambda}]_{i,a_{1}},[\mathbf{\Lambda}]_{a_{1},a_{2}},\dots,[\mathbf{\Lambda}]_{a_{k-1},j}\big)
in which all terms are non-zero. Next, we prove the two cases as follows.

Case 1:

Since
\mathds{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}
, we have
[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}]_{i,a_{1}}=[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}]_{a_{1},a_{2}}=\dots=[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}]_{a_{k-1},j}=1
. By the definition of
\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{pa}}}
, this means that there is a path
j\rightarrow a_{k-1}\rightarrow\dots\rightarrow a_{1}\rightarrow i
in
\mathcal{G}
, which implies that
j\in{\rm{an}}(i)
and
[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}]_{i,j}=1
.
Therefore, we conclude that

\big[(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}\big]_{i,j}\neq 0\quad\implies\quad[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}]_{i,j}=1\ .

(176)

Hence,
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L}^{-1})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{an}}}
.

Case 2:

Since
\mathds{1}(\mathbf{\Lambda})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}
, we have
[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,a_{1}}=[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{a_{1},a_{2}}=\dots=[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{a_{k-1},j}=1
. By the definition of
\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}
, this means that

\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\subseteq{\rm{ch}}(a_{1})\subset\dots\subset{\rm{ch}}(a_{k-1})\subset{\rm{ch}}(j)\ ,

(177)

which implies
[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,j}=1
since
\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(i)\subseteq\overline{{\rm{ch}}}(j)
. Therefore, we conclude that if

\big[(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}+\mathbf{\Lambda})^{-1}\big]_{i,j}\neq 0\quad\implies\quad[\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,j}=1\ .

(178)

Hence,
\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\preccurlyeq\mathds{1}(\mathbf{L}^{-1})\preccurlyeq\mathbf{L}_{{\rm{sur}}}
.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
16

Proof of the scaling consistency.

First, by Theorem
14
, encoder estimate
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
at the end of Stage L1 of Algorithm
1
satisfies

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{H}}\cdot\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{L}\ ,

(179)

where
\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}
is the permutation matrix of the intervention order
(I^{1},\dots,I^{n})
,
\mathbf{L}
has non-zero diagonal entries and satisfies
\mathbf{L}_{i,j}=0
for all
j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
. We will show that the output of Stage L3 of Algorithm
1
for hard interventions satisfies that

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\big([\hat{\mathbf{H}}\cdot\mathbf{G}]_{m}\big)=\mathbf{e}_{I^{m}}^{\top}\ ,\quad\forall m\in[n]\ ,

(180)

which will imply scaling consistency as

\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}})=\hat{\mathbf{H}}\cdot\mathbf{X}=\hat{\mathbf{H}}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm s}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,

(181)

where
\mathbf{C}_{\rm s}
is a constant diagonal matrix with nonzero diagonal entries. We prove this as follows.

First, consider the topological order
\pi
of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
. By Theorem
14
, transitive closures of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{G}
are the same under relabeling of the nodes with permutation
(1,\dots,n)\rightarrow(I^{1},\dots,I^{n})
. Then, since
(1,\dots,n)
is assumed to be topological order of
\mathcal{G}
,
\rho\triangleq(I^{\pi_{1}},\dots,I^{\pi_{n}})
is also a topological order of
\mathcal{G}
. According to this notation, in the
\pi_{t}
-th environment, node
\rho_{t}
is intervened.

Next, since
\rho_{1}
has no parent in
\mathcal{G}
, by Theorem
14
we already know that
\hat{Z}_{\pi_{1}}=b_{1}\cdot Z_{\rho_{1}}
for some constant
b_{1}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. Consider
m=\pi_{k}
step of the algorithm, and assume that for all
t\in[k-1]
, the updated encoder rows satisfy

\hat{Z}_{\pi_{t}}=\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{\pi_{t}}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}=b_{t}\cdot Z_{\rho_{t}}\ ,

(182)

for some constant
b_{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We will show that the update in step
m=\pi_{k}
will ensure that
\hat{Z}_{\pi_{k}}
also satisfies (
182
). Before the update, by Theorem
14
we know that
\hat{Z}_{\pi_{k}}
is a linear function of
Z_{\rho_{k}}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{k})}
. Then, using (
182
), we have

\hat{Z}_{\pi_{k}}=c_{0}\cdot Z_{\rho_{k}}+\mathbf{c}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{k})}\ ,

(183)

for
|\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{k})|
-dimensional vector
\mathbf{c}
and non-zero constant
c_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
. We will use Proposition
15
in environment
\mathcal{E}^{\pi_{k}}
. For brevity, using
m=\pi_{k}
and letting
\mathbf{V}\triangleq\hat{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{k})}
,

\hat{Z}^{m}_{m}=c_{0}\cdot Z^{m}_{\rho_{k}}+\mathbf{c}\cdot\mathbf{V}\ .

(184)

Since node
\rho_{k}
is intervened in environment
\mathcal{E}^{m}
, by Proposition
15
, we know that
Z^{m}_{\rho_{k}}\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathbf{V}
, which implies that

{\rm Cov}(Z^{m}_{\rho_{k}},\mathbf{V})=\boldsymbol{0}_{1\times|\mathbf{V}|}\ .

(185)

Consider a
|\mathbf{V}|
-dimensional row vector
\mathbf{u}
and let

Y=\big(\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)}\big)\cdot\mathbf{X}^{m}=\hat{Z}^{m}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{V}\ .

(186)

Note that
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{c}
would yield that
Y=c_{0}\cdot Z^{m}_{\rho_{k}}
, and subsequently
Y\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathbf{V}
. On the other hand,
Y\perp\!\!\!\perp\mathbf{V}
implies that

\boldsymbol{0}_{1\times|\mathbf{V}|}={\rm Cov}(Y,\mathbf{V})={\rm Cov}(\hat{Z}^{m}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{V},\mathbf{V})\ ,

(187)

which has a unique solution

\mathbf{u}={\rm Cov}(\hat{Z}^{m}_{m},\mathbf{V})\cdot\big({\rm Cov}(\mathbf{V})\big)^{-1}\ .

(188)

i.e., linear minimum mean square error (LMMSE) estimator. Finally,
{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{V})
is invertible since the causal relationships among the entries in
\hat{Z}^{m}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{k})}
are not deterministic. Using this row vector
\mathbf{u}
, we update

\hat{\mathbf{H}}_{m}\leftarrow\mathbf{H}_{m}-\mathbf{u}\cdot\mathbf{H}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{k})}\ ,

(189)

and achieve
\hat{Z}_{m}=c_{0}\cdot Z_{\rho_{k}}
. Therefore, (
182
) holds for
t=k
as well, and by induction we obtain

\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=\hat{\mathbf{H}}\cdot\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm s}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,

(190)

for a diagonal matrix
\mathbf{C}_{\rm s}
with nonzero diagonal entries.
\square

Proof of the perfect DAG recovery.

We show that the graph construction process in (
59
) achieves perfect DAG recovery as follows. First, using Lemma
8
and (
190
), we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})=(\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm s})^{-\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm s}^{-\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\ .

(191)

Subsequently, using the fact that
\mathbf{C}_{\rm s}
is a diagonal matrix, for all
i,m\in[n]
we obtain

\displaystyle\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})\big]_{i}\neq 0\;\;\iff\;\;\big[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{i}}\neq 0\ .

(192)

Then, Lemma
7
and (
59
), we have

\displaystyle I^{i}\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})\;

\displaystyle\iff\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})\big|\Big]_{I^{i}}\neq 0\;\iff\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})\big|_{i}\Big]\;\iff\;i\in\hat{}\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(m)\ .

(193)

This concludes the proof that
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{G}
are related through a graph isomorphism by permutation
\mathcal{I}
, which denotes the intervention order.
\square

B.3
Proof of Theorem
18

Latent graph estimation.

We continue from the intuitions provided in Section
5.4
and prove the perfect recovery of the latent graph as follows. First, recall that output
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
of Algorithm
1
for soft interventions satisfies that transitive closures of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
and
\mathcal{G}
are the same under permutation
(I^{1},\dots,I^{n})
. Since
(1,\dots,n)
is assumed to be a topological order of
\mathcal{G}
,
\rho\triangleq(I^{\pi_{1}},\dots,I^{\pi_{n}})
is also a topological order of
\mathcal{G}
. Then, for any
k=\pi_{u}
and
t=\pi_{v}\in\{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{u-1}\}
, (
64
) becomes

\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{t})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{k})\big)=|{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\cap{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{v})|+\mathds{1}\big\{\rho_{v}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\big\}\ .

(194)

By induction, we will prove that the parent sets generated by Algorithm
2
satisfy

\pi_{u}\in\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{v})\;\iff\;\rho_{u}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{v})\ .

(195)

At the base case
, consider
k=\pi_{2}
. The only possible parent is
t=\pi_{1}
. Since
\rho_{1}
is a root node in
\mathcal{G}
, (
194
) implies

\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{1}})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{2}})\big)=\mathds{1}\{\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{2})\}\ .

(196)

Since
\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{1})=\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{2})=\emptyset
at this step, the algorithm adds
\pi_{1}
to
\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{2})
if and only if
\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{2})
, and (
195
) is satisfied for
k=\pi_{2}
. Next,
as the induction step
, assume that the algorithm output
\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)
satisfies (
195
) for
k\in\{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{u-1}\}
. We prove that
k=\pi_{u}
also satisfies the condition by induction again. At the base case, consider
t=\pi_{1}
. Similar to the previous base case, (
194
) implies

\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{1}})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{u}})\big)=\mathds{1}\{\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\}\ ,

(197)

and since
\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{u})
does not contain
\pi_{1}
yet, the algorithm adds
\pi_{1}
to
\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{u})
if and only if
\rho_{1}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})
. Next, as the induction step, assume that for
\pi_{j}\in\{\pi_{1},\dots,\pi_{v-1}\}
where
v<u
, the algorithm correctly identified the parents, i.e.,
\pi_{j}\in\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{u})
if and only if
\rho_{j}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})
. Consider
k=\pi_{u}
and
t=\pi_{v}
, for which (
194
) implies

\dim\big({\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{v}})\cap{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\pi_{u}})\big)=|{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\cap{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{v})|+\mathds{1}\{\rho_{v}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\}\ .

(198)

By the induction hypothesis, we know that
|\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{u})\cap\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{v})|=|{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})\cap{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{v})|
. Therefore, the algorithm adds
\pi_{v}
to
\hat{\rm{pa}}(\pi_{v})
if and only if
\rho_{v}\in{\rm{pa}}(\rho_{u})
. Therefore, by the inner induction,
\hat{\rm{pa}}(k)
satisfies (
195
). Then, by the outer induction, (
195
) is satisfied for all parent set estimates, which concludes the proof of perfect graph recovery.
\square

Markov property of consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents.

Let
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
denote
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{X};\hat{\mathbf{H}})
. We further investigate the properties of consistency up to mixing with surrounding parents to prove that
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
is Markov with respect to
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
. Using the results of the encoder estimation step, we have

\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}\cdot\mathbf{Z}\ ,

(199)

where
\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}
has non-zero diagonal entries and satisfies
[\mathbf{C}_{\rm sur}]_{i,j}=0
for all
j\notin\overline{{\rm{sur}}}(i)
. Recall the following SCM specified in (
6
)

Z_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)},N_{i})\ .

(200)

Let
\tau
be the permutation that maps
\{1,\dots,n\}
to
\mathcal{I}
, i.e.,
I^{\tau_{i}}=i
for all
i\in[n]
. Since
{\rm{sur}}(i)\subseteq{\rm{pa}}(i)
, (
199
) implies

\displaystyle\hat{Z}_{\tau_{i}}

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,i}\cdot Z_{i}+\sum_{j\in{\rm{sur}}(i)}[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,j}\cdot Z_{j}

(201)

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,i}\cdot f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)},N_{i})+\sum_{j\in{\rm{sur}}(i)}[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}]_{i,j}\cdot Z_{j}\ ,

(202)

which is a function of
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
and
N_{i}
. To prove Markov property, we need to specify
Z_{\tau_{i}}
in terms of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\tau_{i})}
. To this end, it suffices to show that for any
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
,
Z_{k}
is a function of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}}(\tau_{i})
. Using (
199
), we have

\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\tau}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{Z}}\ .

(203)

By Lemma
34
, we know that
[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}^{-1}]_{i,j}=0
if
j\notin{\rm{sur}}(i)
for distinct
i
and
j
. Subsequently, as counterpart of (
201
), we have

Z_{k}=[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}^{-1}]_{k,k}\cdot\hat{Z}_{\tau_{k}}+\sum_{j\in{\rm{sur}}(k)}[\mathbf{C}_{{\rm{sur}}}^{-1}]_{k,j}\cdot\hat{Z}_{\tau_{j}}\ .

(204)

Since
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
, we have
\tau_{k}\in{\rm{pa}}(\tau_{i})
and
\hat{Z}_{\tau_{k}}
is in
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\tau_{i})}
. Note that if
j\in{\rm{sur}}(k)
,
j
is also in
{\rm{pa}}(i)
. Therefore, every term in the RHS of (
204
) belongs to
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\tau_{i})}
and
Z_{k}
is a function of
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\tau_{i})}
. Then, using (
202
), we know that
\hat{Z}_{\tau_{i}}
is a function of only
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}_{\hat{\rm{pa}}(\tau_{i})}
and
N_{i}
, which concludes the proof that
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
is Markov with respect to
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
.

Appendix C
Proofs of the Results for General Transformations

The proof of Theorem
26
is already given in Section
6.2
. In this section, we first prove Theorem
27
, that is identifiability for coupled interventions without observational environment. Then, we prove the results in Section
6.3
related to the uncoupled interventions.

C.1
Proof of Theorem
27

We will show that if
p(\mathbf{Z})
is adjacency-faithful to
\mathcal{G}
and the latent causal model is an additive noise model, then we can recover
\mathcal{G}
without having access to observational environment
\mathcal{E}^{0}
.
Let
\tau
be the permutation that maps
\{1,\dots,n\}
to
\mathcal{I}
, i.e.,
I^{\tau_{i}}=i
for all
i\in[n]
and
\mathbf{P}_{\tau}
to denote the permutation matrix that corresponds to
\tau
, i.e.,

\displaystyle[\mathbf{P}_{\tau}]_{i,m}=\begin{cases}1\ ,&m=\rho_{i}\ ,\\
0\ ,&\text{else}\ .\end{cases}

(205)

By Lemma
7
(iv), true latent score changes across
\{\mathcal{E}^{\tau_{i}},\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{\tau_{j}}\}
gives us
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)
for
i\neq j
. First, we use the perfect latent recovery result to show that Lemma
7
(iv) also applies to estimated latent score changes. Denote
f=\hat{h}\circ g
. Using score transform between
\mathbf{Z}
and
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}
in
(
40
)
, and recalling
\mathds{1}\{J_{f}^{-1}\}=\mathbf{P}_{\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{i}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};\hat{h})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{j}}(\hat{\mathbf{z}};\hat{h})\big]_{\tau_{k}}

\displaystyle=\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\tau_{k}}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\tau_{i}}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\tau_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\big]

(206)

\displaystyle=\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\tau_{k},k}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\tau_{i}}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\tau_{j}}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{k}\ .

(207)

Using
\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\tau_{k},k}\neq 0
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{i}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{j}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{\tau_{k}}\Big]\neq 0\quad\iff\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\tau_{i}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\tau_{j}}(\mathbf{Z})\big|_{\tau_{k}}\Big]\neq 0\ .

(208)

Hence, by Lemma
7
(iv),

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{i}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{j}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{\tau_{k}}\Big]\neq 0\quad\iff\quad k\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)\ .

(209)

Let us denote the graph
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}
that is related to
\mathcal{G}
by permutation
\tau
, i.e.,
i\in{\rm{pa}}(j)
if and only if
\tau_{i}\in{\rm{pa}}_{\tau}(\tau_{j})
for which
{\rm{pa}}_{\tau}(\tau_{j})
denotes the parents of node
\tau_{j}
in
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}
. Using (
209
), we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{i}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\tau_{j}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{h})\big|_{\tau_{k}}\Big]\neq 0\quad\iff\quad\tau_{k}\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}_{\tau}(\tau_{i},\tau_{j})\ .

(210)

In the rest of the proof, we will show how to obtain
\{{\rm{pa}}_{\tau}(i):i\in[n]\}
using
\{\overline{{\rm{pa}}}_{\tau}(i,j):i,j\in[n],\;i\neq j\}
. Since
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}
is a graph isomorphism of
\mathcal{G}
, this problem is equivalent to obtaining
\{{\rm{pa}}(i):i\in[n]\}
using
\{\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j):i,j\in[n],\;i\neq j\}
. Note that
\hat{Z}_{i}
(which corresponds to node
i
in
\mathcal{G}_{\tau}
) is intervened in environments
\mathcal{E}^{i}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{i}
. We denote the set of root nodes by

\mathcal{K}\triangleq\{i\in[n]:{\rm{pa}}(i)=\emptyset\}\ ,

(211)

and also define

\mathcal{B}_{i}\triangleq\,\bigcap_{j\neq i}\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)\ ,\quad\forall i\in[n]\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathcal{B}\triangleq\{i:|\mathcal{B}_{i}|=1\}\ .

(212)

Note that
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Hence,
|\mathcal{B}_{i}|=1
implies that
i
is a root node. We investigate the graph recovery in three cases.

1.

|\mathcal{B}|\geq 3
: For any node
i\in[n]
, we have

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}\subseteq\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{i\}}\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\cup\Big\{\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{i\}}\{j\}\Big\}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\ .

(213)

Note that, the last equality is due to
\textstyle\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\{i\}}\{j\}=\emptyset
since there are at least two root nodes excluding
i
. Then,
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
for all
i\in[n]
and we are done.

2.

|\mathcal{B}|=2
: The two nodes in
\mathcal{B}
are root nodes. If there were at least three root nodes, we would have at least three nodes in
\mathcal{B}
. Hence, the two nodes in
\mathcal{B}
are the only root nodes. Subsequently, every
i\notin\mathcal{B}
is also not in
\mathcal{K}
and we have

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{i}\subseteq\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{K}}\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\cup\Big\{\bigcap_{j\in\mathcal{K}}\{j\}\Big\}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\ .

(214)

Hence,
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
for every non-root node
i
and we already have the two root nodes in
\mathcal{B}
, which completes the graph recovery.

3.

|\mathcal{B}|\leq 1
: First, consider all
(i,j)
pairs such that
|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)|=2
. For such an
(i,j)
pair, at least one of the nodes is a root node, otherwise
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)
would contain a third node. Using these pairs, we identify all root nodes as follows. Note that a hard intervention on node
i
makes
Z_{i}
independent of all of its non-descendants, and all conditional independence relations are preserved under componentwise diffeomorphisms such as
f
. Then, using the adjacency-faithfulness assumption, we infer that

•

if
\hat{Z}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\hat{Z}_{j}
in
\mathcal{E}^{i}
and
\hat{Z}_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\hat{Z}_{j}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{j}
, then both
i
and
j
are root nodes.

•

if
\hat{Z}_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}\!\!\!\perp\hat{Z}_{j}
in
\mathcal{E}^{i}
, then
i\rightarrow j
and
i
is a root node.

•

if
\hat{Z}_{i}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.75pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.625pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 1.875pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\perp}}}\!\!\!\perp\hat{Z}_{j}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{j}
, then
j\rightarrow i
and
j
is a root node.

This implies that we can determine whether
i
and
j
nodes are root nodes by using at most two independence tests. Hence, we identify all root nodes by using at most
n
independence tests. We also know that there are at most two root nodes. If we have two root nodes, then
\mathcal{B}_{i}=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
for all non-root nodes, and the graph is recovered. If we have only one root node
i
, then for any
j\neq i
we have

\displaystyle\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{j}\subseteq\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i,j)=\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)\cup\{i\}\ .

(215)

Finally, if
\hat{Z}_{j}\perp\!\!\!\perp\hat{Z}_{i}\mid\{\hat{Z}_{\ell}:\ell\in\mathcal{B}_{j}\setminus\{i\}\}
in
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}^{j}
, we have
i\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)
due to adjacency-faithfulness. Otherwise, we conclude that
i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)
. Hence, an additional
(n-1)
conditional independence tests ensure the recovery of all
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(j)
sets, and the graph recovery is complete.

C.2
Proof of Lemma
28

Let us start by scrutinizing the constraints. For the true encoder
g^{-1}
, Lemma
7
gives us

\displaystyle[\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})]_{i,m}\neq 0\;\;

\displaystyle\iff\;\;i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{m})\ ,

(216)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(g^{-1})]_{i,m}\neq 0\;\;

\displaystyle\iff\;\;i\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(\tilde{I}^{m})\ .

(217)

Therefore, using
\tilde{\mathcal{I}}=\sigma\circ\mathcal{I}
, we have
\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(g^{-1})\}=\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})\}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\sigma}
. Note that acyclicity of graph
\mathcal{G}
also implies that
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})\}\odot\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})^{\top}\}=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
.

Next, note that
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)
is equal to the true score change matrix
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1})
defined for the coupled interventions, which satisfies
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)\}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}^{\top}
.
Let
h^{*}=\big[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)\big]^{\top}\cdot g^{-1}
. Since
\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)
is a scaled permutation matrix, using (
77
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*};\sigma)

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)]^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\ ,

(218)

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}(h^{*})

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)]^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})

(219)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)]^{-1}\cdot\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(g^{-1})\ .

(220)

Thus, using
\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(g^{-1})\}=\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})\}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\sigma}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\}=\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}\cdot\mathbf{P}_{\sigma}\ ,

(221)

which satisfies the first constraint. Also, using
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h)\}=\mathbf{P}_{\sigma}\cdot\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h)\}
,

\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}=\mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cdot\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(g^{-1})\}\ .

(222)

Therefore, we have
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}\odot\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})^{\top}\}=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
. Finally,
(h^{*})^{-1}\triangleq g\cdot\big[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(g^{-1};\sigma)\big]^{-\top}
satisfies
(h^{*})^{-1}\circ h(\mathbf{X})=\mathbf{X}
. Hence,
(h^{*},\sigma)
minimizes the objective in (
92
) at value zero.

\square

C.3
Proof of Lemma
29

We will prove the desired result by contradiction. Suppose that
(h^{*},\pi)
is a solution to the optimization problem specified in (
92
) and let
\pi\neq\sigma
. Denote
f=h^{*}\circ g
, so we have
\hat{\mathbf{Z}}=f(\mathbf{Z})
. If
\pi_{m}=\sigma_{m}
for some
m\in[n]
, this means
I^{m}=\tilde{I}^{\pi_{m}}=\ell
for some node
\ell\in[n]
. We follow the same approach in the proof of Theorem
23
. Specifically, using score difference transformation property in (
40
) and one-sparse property of
[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{m}}(\mathbf{Z})]
via Lemma
7
(iii), we have

\displaystyle[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*})]_{i,m}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{\pi_{m}}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}})\big|_{i}\Big]

(223)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{i}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{m}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]\Big|\bigg]

(224)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{\ell,i}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{m}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{\ell}\Big]\ .

(225)

By definition of
h^{*}
,
[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*})]_{i,m}=0
for all
i\neq m
. Also, interventional discrepancy between
q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
and
\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{\ell})
implies that
\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{m}}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell}\neq 0
except for a null set. Then,
[\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h^{*})]_{i,m}=0
implies that
\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell,i}=0
except for a null set. Since
J_{f}^{-1}
is a continuous function, this implies that
\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell,i}=0
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Furthermore, since
J_{f}^{-1}
is invertible for all
\mathbf{z}
, none of its columns can be a zero vector. Hence, for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{\ell,m}\neq 0
. To summarize, if
\pi_{m}=\sigma_{m}
, then

\displaystyle\forall\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;\;\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{I^{m},i}\neq 0\quad\iff\quad i=m\ .

(226)

Next, consider the set of
mismatched nodes

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\triangleq\{I^{m}:I^{m}\neq\tilde{I}^{\pi_{m}}\}\ .

(227)

Let
I^{a}\in\mathcal{A}
be a non-descendant of all the other nodes in
\mathcal{A}
. There exist nodes
I^{b},I^{c}\in\mathcal{A}
, not necessarily distinct, such that

I^{a}=\tilde{I}^{\pi_{b}}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad I^{c}=\tilde{I}^{\pi_{a}}\ .

(228)

In four steps, we will show that
[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})]_{a,b}\neq 0
and
[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})]_{b,a}\neq 0
, which violates the constraint
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}\odot\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}^{\top}(h^{*})\}=\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}
and will conclude the proof by contradiction. Before giving the steps, we provide the following argument which we repeatedly use in the rest of the proof. For any continuous function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})\big|\Big]\neq 0\;\;

\displaystyle\iff\;\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\big|\Big]\neq 0\ ,

(229)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})\big|\Big]\neq 0\;\;

\displaystyle\iff\;\;{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{b}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\big|\Big]\neq 0\ .

(230)

First, suppose that
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})|\big]\neq 0
. Then, there exists an open set
\Psi\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for which
f(\mathbf{z})\neq 0
for all
z\in\Psi
. Due to interventional discrepancy between, there exists an open set within
\Psi
for which
[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{a}}\neq 0
. This implies that

{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\big|\Big]\neq 0

(231)

For the other direction, suppose that
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|f(\mathbf{Z})\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\big|\Big]\neq 0
, which implies that there exists an open set
\Psi
for which both
f(\mathbf{z})
and
[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{z})]_{I^{a}}
are non-zero. Then,
{\mathbb{E}}\big[|f(\mathbf{Z})|\big]\neq 0
, and we have (
229
). Similarly, due to
\tilde{I}^{\pi_{b}}=I^{a}
and interventional discrepancy, we obtain (
230
).

Step 1: Show that
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{a},a}\big|\Big]\neq 0
.

First, using (
40
) and Lemma
7
(i), we have

\displaystyle\big[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\big]_{a,a}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{a}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]\Big|\bigg]

(232)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\sum_{I^{j}\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{a})}\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j},a}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j}}\Big|\bigg]\ .

(233)

Note that
\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{a})\cap\mathcal{A}=\{I^{a}\}
since
I^{a}
is non-descendant of the other nodes in
\mathcal{A}
. Consider
I^{j}\in{\rm{pa}}(I^{a})
, which implies that
I^{j}\notin\mathcal{A}
and
I^{j}=\tilde{I}^{\pi_{j}}
. By (
226
), we have
[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{j},a}=0
. Then, (
233
) becomes

\big[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\big]_{a,a}={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a},a}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\Big|\bigg]\neq 0\ ,

(234)

since diagonal entries of
\mathbf{D}(h^{*})
are non-zero due to the last constraint in (
92
). Then, (
229
) implies that
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{a},a}\big|\Big]\neq 0
.

Step 2: Show that
\big[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\big]_{a,b}\neq 0
.

Next, we use
I^{a}=\tilde{I}^{\pi_{b}}
and Lemma
7
(i) to obtain

\displaystyle\big[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\big]_{a,b}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{a}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{b}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]\Big|\bigg]

(235)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\sum_{I^{j}\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{a})}\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j},a}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{b}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j}}\Big|\bigg]

(236)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a},a}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{b}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\Big|\bigg]\ .

(237)

Using (
230
) and Step 1 result, we have
\big[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\big]_{a,b}\neq 0
.

Step 3: Show that
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{a},b}\big|\Big]\neq 0
.

Using (
40
) and Lemma
7
(i), we have

\displaystyle\big[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\big]_{b,b}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{b}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{\boldsymbol{s}}^{\pi_{b}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]\Big|\bigg]

(238)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\sum_{I^{j}\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{a})}\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j},b}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{s}^{\pi_{b}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j}}\Big|\bigg]

(239)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{a},b}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-\tilde{s}^{\rho_{c}}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\Big|\bigg]\ .

(240)

Since
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}=\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\}
, the diagonal entry
\big[\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\big]_{b,b}
is non-zero. Then, using (
230
) we have
{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})]_{I^{a},b}\big|\Big]\neq 0
.

Step 4: Show that
\big[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\big]_{b,a}\neq 0
.

Next, we use
I^{a}=\tilde{I}^{\pi_{b}}
and Lemma
7
(i) to obtain

\displaystyle\big[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\big]_{b,a}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-\top}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{b}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]\Big|\bigg]

(241)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\sum_{I^{j}\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(I^{a})}\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j},b}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{j}}\Big|\bigg]

(242)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\big[J_{f}^{-1}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a},b}\cdot\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{Z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{a}(\mathbf{Z})\big]_{I^{a}}\Big|\bigg]\ .

(243)

Using (
229
) and Step 3 result, we have
[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})]_{b,a}\neq 0
.

Finally, using the constraint
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}=\mathds{1}\{\tilde{\mathbf{D}}(h^{*})\}
, Step 2 implies that
[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})]_{a,b}\neq 0
. Combining with Step 4 result, we have
[\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\odot\mathbf{D}^{\top}(h^{*})]_{a,b}\neq 0
, which violates the last constraint in (
92
). Therefore, if
(h^{*},\pi)
is a minimizer of (
92
), then
\pi
must be the correct coupling
\sigma
.

Appendix D
Analysis of the Assumptions

D.1
Analysis of Assumption
1

In this subsection, we prove Lemma
19
statement, i.e., Assumption
1
is satisfied for additive noise models under hard interventions, as follows.
Consider a hard interventional environment
\mathcal{E}^{m}
and let
I^{m}=i
be the intervened node. Recall (
29
), which becomes

[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}=\big[{\boldsymbol{s}}(\mathbf{z})-{\boldsymbol{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]_{k}=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{k}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

(244)

for the hard intervention on node
i
and parent node
k\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
. Then, the ratio in Assumption
1
is given by

\frac{[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})]_{k}}{[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})]_{i}-[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q(z_{i})]_{i}}=\frac{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}}{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}}\ .

(245)

We will prove the desired result by contradiction. Assume that there exists a non-zero constant
c\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}=c\cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
.

Additive noise model.

The additive noise model for node
i
is given by
Z_{i}=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}
as specified in (
7
). When node
i
is hard intervened,
Z_{i}
is generated according to
Z_{i}=\bar{N}_{i}
in which
\bar{N}_{i}
denotes the exogenous noise term for the interventional model. Then, denoting the pdfs of
N_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
by
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
, respectively, we have

\displaystyle p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=p_{N}(z_{i}-f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad q_{i}(z_{i})=q_{N}(z_{i})\ .

(246)

Denote the score functions associated with
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
by

\displaystyle r_{p}(u)\triangleq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log p_{N}(u)=\frac{p_{N}^{\prime}(u)}{p_{N}(u)}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad r_{q}(u)\triangleq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log q_{N}(u)=\frac{q_{N}^{\prime}(u)}{q_{N}(u)}\ .

(247)

Define
n_{i}
and
\bar{n}_{i}
as the realizations of
N_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
when
Z_{i}=z_{i}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}=\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
. Then, we have
\bar{n}_{i}=n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
. Using (
246
) and (
247
), we can express (
245
) as

\displaystyle\frac{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}}{[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}}=\frac{-\partialderivative{f_i(\bz_{\Pa(i)})}{z_k}\cdot r_{p}(n_{i})}{r_{p}(n_{i})-r_{q}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}\ .

(248)

Then,
[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}=c\cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}
for all
\mathbf{z}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
becomes

c\cdot\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}+1=\frac{r_{q}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}{r_{p}(n_{i})}\ .

(249)

We scrutinize (
249
) in two cases.

Case 1:
\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}
is constant.

In this case, let
\eta=\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}
for some
\eta\in{\mathbb{R}}
for all
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
. Then, the RHS of (
249
) is also constant for all
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
and
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Fix a realization
n_{i}=n_{i}^{*}
and note that
f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
needs to be constant, denoted by
\delta^{*}
, for all
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
. However, this implies that
\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}=0
, and we have
r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})=r_{p}(n_{i})
for all
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. This implies that
p_{N}(n_{i})=\eta^{*}q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
for some constant
\eta^{*}
. Since
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
are pdfs, the only choice is
\eta^{*}=1
and
p_{N}(n_{i})=q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
, then
p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{i}(z_{i})
, which contradicts the premise that an intervention changes the causal mechanism of the target node
i
.

Case 2:
\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})}{\partial z_{k}}
is not constant.

In this case, note that LHS of (
249
) is not a function of
n_{i}
. Then, taking the derivative of both sides with respect to
n_{i}
and rearranging, we obtain

\frac{r_{p}^{\prime}(n_{i})}{r_{p}(n_{i})}=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}{r_{q}(n_{i}+f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))}\ ,\quad\forall(n_{i},\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ .

(250)

Next, consider a fixed realization
n_{i}=n_{i}^{*}
, and denote the value of LHS by
\alpha
. Since
f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
is continuous and not constant, its image contains an open interval
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Denoting
u\triangleq f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
, we have
