\alpha=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}{r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}\ ,\quad\forall u\in\Theta\ .

(251)

The only solution to this equality is that
r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)
is an exponential function,
r_{q}(u)=k_{1}\exp(\alpha u)
over interval
u\in\Theta
. Since
r_{q}
is an analytic function that equals to an exponential function over an interval, it is exponential over entire
\mathbb{R}
. This implies that the pdf
q_{N}(u)
is of the form
q_{N}(u)=k_{2}\exp((k_1/\alpha) \exp(\alpha u))
. However, this cannot be a valid pdf since its integral over
{\mathbb{R}}
diverges. Then,
[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{k}=c\cdot[{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}
cannot be true, which concludes the proof.

D.2
Analysis of Assumption
2

In this section, we establish the necessary and sufficient conditions under which Assumption
2
holds for additive models. Furthermore, we show that a large class of non-linear models in the latent space satisfy these conditions, including the two-layer neural networks. We have focused on such NNs since they effectively approximate continuous functions
(
Cybenko 1989
)
. Readily, the necessary and sufficient conditions can be investigated for other choices of non-linear functions.

D.2.1
Interpreting Assumption
2

The implication of Assumption
2
is that the effect of an intervention is not lost in any linear combination of the varying coordinates of the scores. To formalize this, for each node
i\in[n]
, we define

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i}\triangleq\{\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\;:\;\exists j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\;\;\mbox{such that }\;c_{j}\neq 0\}\ ,

(252)

Let
I^{m}=i
. Note that
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}
is positive semi-definite and
[\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}]_{j,k}=0
for all
(j,k)\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\times\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
. Therefore, for any
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
,

\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\cdot\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{E}\big[(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m})\cdot(\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m})^{\top}\big]\neq 0\ ,

(253)

since
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}
has rank
|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)|
. The reverse direction also holds true, i.e., if
\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}
has rank less than
|\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)|
, then there exists
\mathbf{c}\mathcal{C}_{i}
which makes
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\cdot\mathbf{c}=0
. Therefore, Assumption
2
is equivalent to the following statement:

\mathbf{E}\big[|\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}|\big]\neq 0\ ,\quad\forall\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\ .

(254)

Necessary and sufficient conditions.

Consider the additive noise model for node
i

\displaystyle Z_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}\ ,

(255)

as specified in (
7
). When node
i
is soft intervened,
Z_{i}
is generated according to

\displaystyle Z_{i}

\displaystyle=\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+\bar{N}_{i}\ ,

(256)

in which
\bar{f}_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
specify the interventional mechanism for node
i
. The following lemma characterizes the necessary and sufficient conditions under which (
254
) (equivalently, Assumption
2
) is satisfied. In this subsection, we use
\varphi
as the shorthand for
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
.

Lemma 0
.

For each node
i\in[n]
consider the following two set of equations for
c\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=0\\
\\
c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\end{array}\right.\ ,\qquad\qquad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ .

Assumption
2
holds if and only if the only all solutions
\mathbf{c}
to (
35
) satisfy
\mathbf{c}\notin\mathcal{C}_{i}
, or based on (
252
), equivalently
c_{j}=0
for all
j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)
.

Proof:
See Appendix
D.2.2
.

To provide some intuition about the conditions in Lemma
35
, we consider a node
i\in[n]
and discuss the conditions in the context of a few examples. Note that by sweeping
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
we generate a continuum of linear equations of the form:

\displaystyle c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=0\ ,\qquad\mbox{and}\qquad c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\ .

(260)

Note that for all
j\not\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
we have
[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)]_{j}=[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)]_{j}=0
. Hence, in finding the solutions to (
260
) only the coordinates
\{j\in\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\}
of
\mathbf{c}
are relevant. Let us define

\displaystyle u(\varphi)\triangleq\nabla_{\varphi}f_{i}(\varphi)\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\bar{u}(\varphi)\triangleq\nabla_{\varphi}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\ ,

(261)

which are the gradients of
f_{i}
and
f_{i}
by considering only the coordinates of
\mathbf{z}
in
\{j\in{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\}
. Accordingly, we also define
\mathbf{b}
by concatenating only the coordinates of
\mathbf{c}
with their indices in
\{j\in{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\}
. Next, consider
w
distinct choices of
\varphi
and denote them by
\{\varphi^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}:t\in[w]\}
. By concatenating the two equations in (
261
) specialized to these realizations, we get the following linear system with
2w
equations and
|{\rm{pa}}(i)|+1
unknown variables.

\displaystyle\underset{\triangleq\,\mathbf{V}\,\in\,{\mathbb{R}}^{2w\times(|{\rm{pa}}(i)|+1)}}{\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}[u(\varphi^{1})]^{\top}&-1\\
[\bar{u}(\varphi^{1})]^{\top}&-1\\
\vdots&\vdots\\
[u(\varphi^{w})]^{\top}&-1\\
[\bar{u}(\varphi^{w})]^{\top}&-1\\
\end{bmatrix}}}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{b}\\
c_{i}\end{bmatrix}=\boldsymbol{0}_{2w}\ .

(262)

When
\mathbf{V}
is full-rank, i.e.,
\rank(\bV)=|{\rm{pa}}(i)|+1
, the system has only the trivial solutions
c_{i}=0
and
\mathbf{b}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Then, we make the following observations.

1.

If
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
are linear functions, the vector spaces generated by
u
and
\bar{u}
have dimensions 1. Subsequently, we always have
\rank(\bV)\leq 2
, rendering an underdetermined system when
|{\rm{pa}}(i)|\geq 2
. Hence, when the maximum degree of
\mathcal{G}
is at least 2, a linear causal model does not satisfy Assumption
2
.

2.

If
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
are quadratic with full-rank matrices, i.e.,
f_{i}(\varphi)=\varphi^{\top}\mathbf{A}\varphi
and
\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=\varphi^{\top}\bar{\mathbf{A}}\varphi
where
\rank(\bA)=\rank(\bar\bA)=|{\rm{pa}}(i)|
, there is a choice of
w\geq|{\rm{pa}}(i)|+1
and realizations
\{\varphi^{t}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}:t\in[w]\}
for which
\rank(\bV)=|{\rm{pa}}(i)|+1
and the system in (
260
) admits only the trivial solutions
a_{i}=0
and
\mathbf{b}=\boldsymbol{0}
. Hence, quadratic causal models satisfy Assumption
2
.

3.

If
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
are two-layer NNs with a sufficiently large number of hidden neurons, they also render a fully determined system, and as a result, they satisfy Assumption
2
.

We investigate the last example in detail as follows. Assume that
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
are two-layer NNs with
|{\rm{pa}}(i)|
inputs,
w_{i}
and
\bar{w}_{i}
hidden nodes, respectively, and with sigmoid activation functions. Denote the weight matrices between input and hidden layers in
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
by
\mathbf{W}^{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{w_{i}\times|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
and
\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\bar{w}_{i}\times|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
, respectively. Furthermore, define
\boldsymbol{\nu}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{w_{i}}
and
\bar{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{w_{i}}
as the weights between the hidden layer and output in
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
, respectively. Finally, define
\nu_{0}
and
\bar{\nu}_{0}
as the bias terms. Hence, we have

\displaystyle f_{i}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\nu}^{\top}\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}^{i}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{w_{i}}\nu_{j}\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}^{i}_{j}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}\ ,

(263)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\bar{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{\top}\cdot\sigma(\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{\bar{w}_{i}}\bar{\nu}_{j}\cdot\sigma(\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i}_{j}\cdot\varphi)+\bar{\nu}_{0}\ ,

(264)

in which activation function
\sigma
is applied element-wise.

Lemma
21

Consider NNs
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
specified in (
263
) and (
264
). If for all
i\in[n]
we have

\displaystyle\max\{\rank(\bW^i)\;,\;\rank(\bar\bW^i)\}=|{\rm{pa}}(i)|\ ,

(265)

then Assumption
2
holds.

Proof:
See Appendix
D.2.3
.

D.2.2
Proof of Lemma
35

We show that for node
I^{m}=i
, the condition in (
254
)

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{m}\big|\Big]\neq 0\ ,\;\;\forall c\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\ ,

(266)

holds if and only if the following continuum of equations admit their solutions
\mathbf{c}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=0\\
\\
c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\end{array}\right.\ ,\qquad\qquad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ ,

in which shorthand
\varphi
is used for
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
. We first note that the condition in (
266
) can be equivalently stated using (
18
) and (
19
) as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\Big|\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\big[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(Z_{i}\mid\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})-\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{i}(Z_{i}\mid\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big]\Big|\bigg]\neq 0\ ,\quad\forall\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}\ .

(270)

Also, using Proposition
32
, (
270
) is equivalent to

\displaystyle\forall\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i},\;\;\exists\mathbf{z}:\quad\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\neq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ .

(271)

The additive noise model for node
i
is given by

\displaystyle Z_{i}

\displaystyle=f_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+N_{i}\ ,

(272)

as specified in (
7
). When node
i
is soft intervened,
Z_{i}
is generated according to

\displaystyle Z_{i}

\displaystyle=\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})+\bar{N}_{i}\ ,

(273)

in which
\bar{f}_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
specify the interventional mechanism for node
i
. Then, denoting the pdfs of
N_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
by
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
, respectively, (
272
) and (
272
) imply that

\displaystyle p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=p_{N}\big(z_{i}-f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{N}\big(z_{i}-\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big)\ .

(274)

Denote the score functions associated with
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
by

\displaystyle r_{p}(u)\triangleq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log p_{N}(u)=\frac{p_{N}^{\prime}(u)}{p_{N}(u)}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad r_{q}(u)\triangleq\frac{{\rm d}}{{\rm d}u}\log q_{N}(u)=\frac{q_{N}^{\prime}(u)}{q_{N}(u)}\ .

(275)

Define
n_{i}
and
\bar{n}_{i}
as the realizations of
N_{i}
and
\bar{N}_{i}
when
Z_{i}=z_{i}
and
\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}=\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
. By defining
\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\triangleq f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})-\bar{f}_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
, we have
\bar{n}_{i}=n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
. Using (
275
) and (
274
), we can express the relevant entries of
\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
as

\displaystyle\big[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big]_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}r_{p}(n_{i})\ ,&j=i\ ,\\
-\partialderivative{f_i(\bz_{\Pa(i)})}{z_j}r_{p}(n_{i})\ ,&j\in{\rm{pa}}(i)\ ,\\
0\ ,&j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\ ,\end{cases}

(276)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad\big[\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\big]_{j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}r_{q}(\bar{n}_{i})\ ,&j=i\ ,\\
-\partialderivative{\bar f_i(\bz_{\Pa(i)})}{z_j}r_{q}(\bar{n}_{i})\ ,&j\in{\rm{pa}}(i)\ ,\\
0\ ,&j\notin\overline{{\rm{pa}}}(i)\ .\end{cases}

(277)

By substituting (
276
)–(
277
) in (
271
) and rearranging the terms, the statement in (
271
) becomes equivalent to following statement. For all
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, there exist
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
such that

\displaystyle r_{p}(n_{i})\cdot\bigg(c_{i}-\sum_{j\in{{\rm{pa}}}(i)}c_{j}\cdot\partialderivative{f_i(\bz_{\Pa(i)})}{z_j}\bigg)\neq r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}))\cdot\bigg(c_{i}-\sum_{j\in{{\rm{pa}}}(i)}c_{j}\cdot\partialderivative{\bar f_i(\bz_{\Pa(i)})}{z_j}\bigg)\ ,

(278)

which by using the shorthand
\varphi
for
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
can be compactly presented as follows. For all
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, there exists
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
such that

\displaystyle r_{p}(n_{i})\cdot\left[c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)\right]\neq r_{q}\big(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi)\big)\cdot\left[c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\right]\ .

(279)

Hence, Assumption
2
is equivalent to the statement in (
279
), which we use for the rest of the proof.

Sufficient condition.

We show that if (
D.2.2
) admit solutions
\mathbf{c}
only in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}
, then the statement in (
279
) holds. By contradiction, assume that there exists
\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that for all
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
,

\displaystyle r_{p}(n_{i})\cdot\left[c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)\right]=r_{q}\big(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi)\big)\cdot\left[c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\right]\ .

(280)

We show that
\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
is also a solution to (
D.2.2
), contradicting the premise. In order to show that
\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
is also a solution to (
D.2.2
), suppose, by contradiction, that (
280
) holds, and there exists
\varphi^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
corresponding to which

\displaystyle(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi^{*})-c^{*}_{i}\neq 0\ .

(281)

Note that
f_{i}
is a continuously differentiable function and also a function of
z_{j}
for all
j\in{\rm{pa}}(i)
. Hence, there exists an open set
\Phi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
for
\varphi
for which
(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi^{*})-c^{*}_{i}
is non-zero everywhere in
\Phi
. Likewise,
r_{p}
cannot constantly be zero over all possible intervals
\Omega
. This is because otherwise, it would have to necessarily be a constant zero function (since it is analytic), which is an invalid score function. Hence, there exists an open interval
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}
over which
r_{p}(n_{i})
is non-zero for all
n_{i}\in\Omega
. Subsequently, the left-hand side of (
280
) is non-zero over the Cartesian product
(n_{i},\varphi)\in\Omega\times\Phi
. This means that if (
280
) is true, then both functions on its right-hand side must be also non-zero over
\Omega\times\Phi
. Hence, by rearranging the terms in (
280
) we have

\displaystyle\frac{r_{q}\big(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi)\big)}{r_{p}(n_{i})}=\frac{c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)}{c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)}\ ,\qquad\forall(n_{i},\varphi)\in\Omega\times\Phi\ .

(282)

In two steps, we show that (
282
) cannot be valid.

Step 1.
First, we show that function
\delta
cannot be a constant over
\Phi
(interval specified above). Suppose the contrary and assume that
\delta(\varphi)=\delta^{*}
for all
\varphi\in\Phi
. Hence, the gradient of
\delta
is zero. Using the definition of
\delta
, this implies that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in\Phi\ .

(283)

Then, by leveraging (
282
), we conclude that
r_{p}(n_{i})=r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
for all
n_{i}\in\Omega
. Since
r_{p}(n_{i})
and
r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
are analytic functions that agree on an open interval of
\mathbb{R}
, they are equal for all
n_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
as well. This implies that
p_{N}(n_{i})=\eta\cdot q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
for some constant
\eta
. Since
p_{N}
and
q_{N}
are pdfs, the only choice is
\eta=1
and
p_{N}(n_{i})=q_{N}(n_{i}+\delta^{*})
, then
p_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=q_{i}(z_{i}\mid\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})
, which contradicts the premise that observational and interventional mechanisms are distinct.

Step 2.
Finally, we will show that (
282
) cannot be true when
\delta
is not a constant function over
\Phi
. Note that the right-hand side of (
282
) is not a function of
n_{i}
. Then, taking the derivative of both sides with respect to
n_{i}
and rearranging, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{r_{p}^{\prime}(n_{i})}{r_{p}(n_{i})}

\displaystyle=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi))}{r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi))}\ ,\quad\forall(n_{i},\varphi)\in\Omega\times\Phi\ .

(284)

Next, consider a fixed realization
n_{i}=n_{i}^{*}
, and denote the value of LHS by
\alpha
. Since
\delta
is continuous and not constant over
\Phi
, its image contains an open interval
\Theta\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Denoting
u\triangleq\delta(\varphi)
, we get

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{r_{q}^{\prime}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}{r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)}\ ,\quad\forall u\in\Theta\ .

(285)

The only solution to this equality is that
r_{q}(n_{i}^{*}+u)
is an exponential function,
r_{q}(u)=k_{1}\exp(\alpha u)
over interval
u\in\Theta
. Since
r_{q}
is an analytic function that equals to an exponential function over an interval, it is exponential over entire
\mathbb{R}
. This implies that the pdf
q_{N}(u)
is of the form
q_{N}(u)=k_{2}\exp((k_1/\alpha) \exp(\alpha u))
. However, this cannot be a valid pdf since its integral over
{\mathbb{R}}
diverges. Hence, (
282
) is not true, and the premise that there exists
\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
corresponding to which (
281
) holds is invalid, concluding that for all
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}
we have
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=c_{i}
. Proving the counterpart identity
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=c_{i}
follows similarly. Therefore, (
280
) implies

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=0\\
\\
c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\end{array}\right.\ ,\qquad\qquad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ ,

which means that we have found a solution to (
D.2.2
) that is not in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}
, contradicting the premise.

Necessary condition.

Assume that Assumption
2
, and equivalently, the statement in (
279
) holds but (
D.2.2
) has a solution
\mathbf{c}^{*}
in
\mathcal{C}_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ .

(289)

Multiplying the left side by
r_{p}(n_{i})
and the right side by
r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi))
, we obtain that for all
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}

\displaystyle r_{p}(n_{i})\cdot\left[c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)\right]=r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi))\cdot\left[c^{*}_{i}-(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\right]=0\ ,

(290)

which implies that for all
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
,
n_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}

\displaystyle r_{p}(n_{i})\cdot\left[c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)\right]=r_{q}(n_{i}+\delta(\varphi))\cdot\left[c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)\right]\ .

(291)

This contradicts (
279
), and equivalently Assumption
2
. Hence, the proof is complete.

D.2.3
Proof of Lemma
21

Approach.

We will use the same argument as at the beginning of the proof of Lemma
35
. Specifically, we will show that for any node
i
and two-layer NNs
f_{i}
and
\bar{f}_{i}
with weight matrices
\mathbf{W}^{i}
and
\bar{\mathbf{W}}^{i}
, the following continuum of equations admit their solutions
\mathbf{c}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{i}
.

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\bar{f}_{i}(\varphi)=0\\
\\
c_{i}-\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=0\end{array}\right.\ ,\qquad\qquad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(i)|}\ ,

in which shorthand
\varphi
is used for
\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}
. Hence, by invoking Lemma
35
, Assumption
2
holds.

Definitions.

Define
d_{i}\triangleq|{\rm{pa}}(i)|
. Since
\max\{\rank(\bW^i),\rank(\bar\bW^i)\}=d_{i}
, without loss of generality, suppose that
\mathbf{W}^{i}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{w_{i}\times d_{i}}
has rank
d_{i}
. The rest of the proof follows similarly for the case of
\rank(\bar\bW^i)=d_{i}
. We use shorthand
\{\mathbf{W},w\}
for
\{\mathbf{W}^{i},w_{i}\}
when it is obvious from context.

Parameterization.

Note that
f_{i}
can be represented by different parameterizations, some containing more hidden nodes than others. Without loss of generality, let
w
be the fewest number of nodes that can represent
f_{i}
. This implies that the entries of
\boldsymbol{\nu}
are non-zero. Otherwise, if
\nu_{i}=0
, we can remove
i
-th hidden node and still have the same
f_{i}
. Similarly, the rows of
\mathbf{W}
are distinct. Otherwise, if there exist rows
\mathbf{W}_{i}=\mathbf{W}_{j}
for distinct
i,j\in[w]
, removing
j
-th hidden node and using
(\nu_{i}+\nu_{j})
in place of
\nu_{i}
results the same function as
f_{i}
with
(w-1)
hidden nodes. Similarly, we have
\mathbf{W}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
for all
i\in[w]
. Otherwise, we have

\nu_{i}\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{i}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}=(\nu_{0}+\frac{\nu_{i}}{2})\ ,

(295)

and by removing
i
-th hidden node and using
(\nu_{0}+\frac{\nu_{i}}{2})
instead of
\nu_{0}
, we reach the same function as
f_{i}
with
(w-1)
hidden nodes. Finally, we have
\mathbf{W}_{i}+\mathbf{W}_{j}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
. Otherwise, we have

\nu_{i}\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{i}\varphi)+\nu_{j}\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{j}\cdot\varphi)+\nu_{0}=(\nu_{i}-\nu_{j})\cdot\sigma(\mathbf{W}_{i}\cdot\varphi)+(\nu_{0}+\nu_{j})\ ,

(296)

and by removing
j
-th hidden node and using
(\nu_{i}-\nu_{j})
instead of
\nu_{i}
and
(\nu_{0}+\nu_{j})
instead of
\nu_{0}
, we reach the same function as
f_{i}
with
(w-1)
hidden nodes. In summary, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\ ,\;\;\nu_{i}\neq 0\ ,\quad\forall i\in[w]\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad\mathbf{W}_{i}\pm\mathbf{W}_{j}\neq\boldsymbol{0}\ ,\quad\forall i,j\in[w]:i\neq j\ .

(297)

We will show that there does not exist
\mathbf{c}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=c_{i}
for all
\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
.
Assume the contrary, and assume there exist
\mathbf{c}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{c}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}f_{i}(\varphi)=c^{*}_{i}\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ .

(298)

This is equivalent to showing that there exists non-zero
\mathbf{b}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
such that

\displaystyle(\mathbf{b}^{*})^{\top}\cdot\nabla_{\varphi}f_{i}(\phi)=c_{i}^{*}\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ .

(299)

Based on (
263
), the gradient of
f_{i}(\varphi)
is

\displaystyle\nabla_{\varphi}f_{i}(\varphi)=\mathbf{W}^{\top}\cdot{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\dot{\sigma}(W\cdot\varphi)\ ,

(300)

where
{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})
is the diagonal matrix with
\boldsymbol{\nu}
as its diagonal elements, and
\dot{\sigma}
is the derivative of the sigmoid function, applied element-wise to its argument. Hence, based on (
298
), the contradiction premise is equivalent to having a non-zero
\mathbf{b}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
such that

\displaystyle\big[\dot{\sigma}(\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi)\big]^{\top}\cdot{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*}=c_{i}\ ,\quad\forall\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ .

(301)

We note that since
\mathbf{W}
is full-rank and
\nu_{i}
is non-zero for all
i\in[w]
, the matrix
{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}
is full-rank as well and it has a trivial null space. Subsequently,
\mathbf{b}^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
is non-zero if and only if
({\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{w}
is non-zero. We will use the following lemma to show that (
301
) cannot be true. This establishes that the contradiction premise is not correct, and completes the proof.

Lemma 0
.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
have non-zero entries with distinct absolute values, i.e.,
u_{i}\neq 0
and
|u_{i}|\neq|u_{j}|
for all
i\neq j
, and
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
be a constant. Then, for every non-zero vector
\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{p}
, there exists
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
[\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\mathbf{u})]^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{c}\neq a
.

Proof:
See Appendix
D.2.4
.

Let us define
\xi\triangleq\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi
. We will show that there exists
\varphi^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
such that
\xi=\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi^{*}
satisfies the conditions in Lemma
36
. Then, using Lemma
36
with the choice of
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi^{*}
,
d={\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*}
, and
a=c_{i}
, we find that there exists
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\displaystyle\big[\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi^{*})\big]^{\top}\cdot{\rm diag}(\boldsymbol{\nu})\cdot\mathbf{W}\cdot\mathbf{b}^{*}\neq c_{i}\ .

(302)

Hence, (
301
) is false since it is violated for
\varphi=\alpha\varphi^{*}
and the proof is completed. We show the existence of such
\varphi^{*}
as follows. We first construct the set of
\varphi
values for which conditions of Lemma
36
on
w
are
not
satisfied. The set in question is the union of the following cases: (i)
\xi_{i}=\mathbf{W}_{i}\cdot\varphi=0
for some
i\in[w]
, (ii)
|\xi_{i}|=|\xi_{j}|
for some distinct
i,j\in[w]
, or equivalently,
(\mathbf{W}_{i}\pm\mathbf{W}_{j})\varphi=0
. Note that
\mathbf{W}_{i}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
and
\mathbf{W}_{i}\pm\mathbf{W}_{j}\neq\boldsymbol{0}
by (
297
). For a non-zero vector
\mathbf{y}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
, the set
\{\varphi\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}\ :\ \mathbf{y}^{\top}\varphi=0\}
is a
(d_{i}-1)
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
. Then, there is
w
number of
(d_{i}-1)
-dimensional subspaces that fall under case (i), and
w(w-1)
number of
(d_{i}-1)
-dimensional subspaces that fall under case (ii). Therefore, there are
w^{2}
lower-dimensional subspaces for which the conditions of Lemma
36
do not hold. However,
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
cannot be covered by a finite number of lower-dimensional subspaces of itself. Therefore, there exists
\varphi^{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{i}}
such that
\xi=\mathbf{W}\cdot\varphi^{*}
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
36
, and the proof is completed.

D.2.4
Proof of Lemma
36

Assume the contrary and suppose that there exists a non-zero
\mathbf{c}
and
\alpha
for a given
\mathbf{u}
. Define the function
g_{u}(\alpha)\triangleq\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\mathbf{u})
. Note that

\displaystyle\dot{\sigma}(x)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-x}+e^{x}}

(303)

is an even analytic function, and its Taylor series expansion at
0
has the domain of convergence
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}:|x|<\frac{\pi}{2}\}
. Thus, for all
\alpha\in(-\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|},\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|})
,

\displaystyle a=g_{u}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}\cdot\dot{\sigma}(\alpha u_{j})=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\gamma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}(\alpha u_{j})^{2i}=\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}(\gamma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}u_{j}^{2i})\alpha^{2i}\ .

(304)

Note that
g_{u}(\alpha)
is a constant function of
\alpha\in(-\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|},\frac{\pi}{2\max_{i}|u_{i}|})
. Thus, its Taylor coefficients, i.e.,
(\gamma_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{p}c_{j}u_{j}^{2i})
, are zero for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
. However, the coefficients of even powers in Taylor expansion of
\dot{\sigma}
, i.e.,
\gamma_{i}
’s, are non-zero
(
Weisstein 2002
)
. Therefore, we have
\sum_{i=1}^{p}(c_{j}u_{j}^{2i})=0
for all
i\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+}
. Next, construct the following system of linear equations,

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}u_{1}^{2}&u_{1}^{4}&\dots&u_{1}^{2w}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\dots&\vdots\\
u_{p}^{2}&u_{p}^{4}&\dots&u_{p}^{2w}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
\vdots\\
c_{p}\end{bmatrix}=\boldsymbol{0}\ .

(305)

This is equivalent to

\displaystyle{\rm diag}\big([u_{1}^{2},\dots,u_{p}^{2}]^{\top}\big)\cdot\underset{{\rm Vandermonde}}{\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}1&u_{1}^{2}&\dots&u_{1}^{2(p-1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\dots&\vdots\\
1&u_{p}^{2}&\dots&u_{p}^{2(p-1)}\end{bmatrix}}}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}c_{1}\\
\vdots\\
c_{p}\end{bmatrix}=\boldsymbol{0}\ .

(306)

Note that the Vandermonde matrix in (
306
) has determinant
\prod_{1\leq i\leq j<p}(u_{i}^{2}-u_{j}^{2})
, which is non-zero since
|u_{i}|\neq|u_{j}|
for
i\neq j
. Multiplying an invertible matrix with a diagonal invertible matrix generates another invertible matrix, and
\mathbf{c}
must be a zero vector which is a contradiction. Hence, there does not exist such
\mathbf{c}
for which
\mathbf{c}^{\top}\cdot\dot{\sigma}(\alpha\mathbf{u})
is constant for every
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

D.3
Assumptions of
(
Zhang et al. 2023
)

We clarify that Assumption 2 of
Zhang et al. 2023
, which is referred to as “linear interventional faithfulness”,
requires
nonlinear SCMs. Subsequently, both of their main results (Theorem 1 and Theorem 2) require this assumption. First, note that
Zhang et al. 2023
implicitly explain that their results are for nonlinear SCMs:
“In general, we show in Appendix B that a large class of non-linear SCMs and soft interventions satisfy this assumption.”
Next, we show that this assumption is violated for linear additive noise models as follows. First, we quote their assumption:

Linear interventional faithfulness

(
Zhang et al. 2023
, Assumption 2)
: Intervention
I
on node
i
satisfies linear interventional faithfulness if for every
j\in{\rm{ch}}(i)\cup\{i\}
such that
{\rm{pa}}(j)\cap{\rm{de}}(i)=\emptyset
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S})\neq\mathbb{P}^{I}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S})
for all constant vectors
\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|}
where
S=[n]\setminus{\rm{de}}(i)
.

Linear SCMs violate linear interventional faithfulness.

Next, we show that linear SCMs violate the above linear interventional faithfulness assumption. Consider an intervention on node
i
, and let
j\in{\rm{ch}}(i)
such that
{\rm{pa}}(i)\cap{\rm{de}}(i)=\emptyset
. Let

Z_{j}=\mathbf{w}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}+N_{j}\ ,

(307)

where
\mathbf{w}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|{\rm{pa}}(j)|}
denotes the weight vector. Note that we have
{\rm{pa}}(j)\subseteq S=[n]\setminus{\rm{de}}(i)
since
{\rm{pa}}(i)\cap{\rm{de}}(i)=\emptyset
. Consider vector
\mathbf{c}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{|S|}
such that
\mathbf{c}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}=-\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{c}_{S\setminus{\rm{pa}}(j)}=\boldsymbol{0}
where
\mathbf{c}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}
denotes the entries corresponding to nodes in
{\rm{pa}}(j)
. Then, we have

Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S}=N_{j}\ ,

(308)

which remains invariant after an intervention on node
i
and yields
\mathbb{P}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S})=\mathbb{P}^{I}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{Z}_{S})
. Therefore, linear SCMs violate linear interventional faithfulness.

Appendix E
Empirical Evaluations: Details and Additional Results

E.1
Implementation Details of LSCALE-I Algorithm

Preprocessing: Dimensionality reduction.

Since
\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{G}\cdot\mathbf{Z}
, we can compute
{\rm im}(\mathbf{G})
using
n
random samples of
\mathbf{X}
almost surely. Specifically,
{\rm im}(\mathbf{G})
equals the column space of the sample covariance matrix of
n
samples of
\mathbf{X}
. In LSCALE-I, as a preprocessing step, we compute this subspace and express samples of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
in this basis. This procedure effectively reduces the dimension of
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}
from
d
to
n
. Then, we perform steps of LSCALE-I using this
n
dimensional observed data.

Implementation details of Algorithm
1
.

In Stage L1 of Algorithm
1
, choosing any
\mathbf{h}\in{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m})
suffices. To make our algorithm deterministic, we choose the top eigenvector of
{\rm col}(\mathbf{R}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m})
that corresponds to the largest eigenvalue. In Stage L2, we use a nonzero threshold
\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}
, i.e.,

\hat{\rm{pa}}(m)\triangleq\Big\{i\neq m:{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big|{\boldsymbol{d}}_{\hat{\mathbf{Z}}}^{m}(\hat{\mathbf{Z}};\hat{\mathbf{H}})\big|_{i}\Big]\geq\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}\Big\}\ .

(309)

In Table
17
, we list all
\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}
values used in the experiments.

Table 17
:
Choice of thresholding parameter
\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}
for latent graph estimation.

Implementation details of Algorithm
2
.

The latent graph estimation part of Algorithm
2
involves rank tests, i.e., determining the dimension of the intersection of column spaces. Therefore, we also need a threshold, denoted by
\lambda_{\rm eigv}
to determine the numerical ranks in practice. In experiments reported in Table
8
, we set
\lambda_{\rm eigv}=0.01
.

Using the algorithms of related work.

For the comparisons in Section
7.3
, we used the shared sources codes of
Squires et al. 2023
8
8

8

https://github.com/csquires/linear-causal-disentanglement-via-interventions
and
Zhang et al. 2023
9
9

9

https://github.com/uhlerlab/discrepancy_vae
.

E.2
Implementation Details of GSCALE-I Algorithm

We perform experiments for the coupled interventions setting, and solve the following optimization problem in Step G2 of GSCALE-I,

\min_{h}\big\|\mathbf{D}_{\rm t}(h)-\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}\big\|_{1,1}+\lambda{\mathbb{E}}\Big[\big\|h^{-1}(h(\mathbf{X}))-\mathbf{X}\big\|_{2}^{2}\Big]\ .

(310)

where
\lambda>0
is a regularization parameter to ensure injectivity. In the following, first, we show why this problem is equivalent to solving (
83
). Then, we describe the computation of the ground truth score differences for
\mathbf{X}
and discuss other implementation details.

Implementation steps.

We use
n_{\rm s}
samples from the observational environment to compute empirical expectations. Since encoder
h
is parameterized by
\mathbf{H}
, we use gradient descent to learn this matrix. To do so, we minimize the loss described above in (
310
). We denote the final parameter estimate by
\mathbf{H}^{*}
and the encoder parameterized by
\mathbf{H}^{*}
by
h^{*}
.

In the simulation results reported in Section
7.2
, we set
\lambda=1
and solve (
310
) using RMSprop optimizer with learning rate
10^{-3}
for
3\times 10^{4}
steps for
n=5
and
4\times 10^{4}
steps for
n=8
. We also use early stopping when the training converges before the maximum number of steps. Recall that the latent graph estimate
\hat{\mathcal{G}}
is constructed using
\mathds{1}\{\mathbf{D}(h^{*})\}
. Similarly to (
309
), We use a threshold
\lambda_{\mathcal{G}}
to obtain the graph from the upper triangular part of
\mathbf{D}(h^{*})
.

Architecture details and hyperparameter selection for image experiments.

18
and

, we list the architecture details and training hyperparameters used in the image experiments in Section
7.5
. Specifically, Table
18
specifies the autoencoder architecture we used to estimate the latent variables, and Table
19
details the classification-based score difference network we trained. Finally, Table
20
lists the first level CNN-based autoencoder we used for image experiments with 2 balls.

E.3
Experimental Methodology Comparison with
(
Zhang et al. 2023
)

One major difference between the LSCALE-I algorithm and the publicly available DiscrepancyVAE implementation is that DiscrepancyVAE tries to learn the intervention effects as an encoding of the intervention labels. Specifically, for each intervention target, the VAE model learns a vector of latent intervention targets (a neural network with softmax activation) to denote which latent node is intervened on. In practice, learning an intervention encoding runs the risk of learning encodings where different interventions affect the same latent node, consequently, having nodes that are not affected by any intervention encoding. We demonstrate this phenomenon for DiscrepancyVAE in Figure
8
, plotting the learned intervention embeddings for each single-node intervention. We observe that despite having exhaustive single-node interventions, the intervention embedding network fails to learn exhaustive intervention targets/effects. Therefore, the learned graph is not immediately interpretable as a DAG on
n
causal latent variables since the learned intervention targets (i.e., node labels) are not surjective.

In contrast, in LSCALE-I, the effect of an intervention is estimated using score function differences. While the distinction may seem small, this approach ensures that the estimated graph’s nodes are always interpretable since score differences between observational and interventional environments directly correspond to the intervention targets.

Figure 8
:
Intervention encoding learned in the sample run in Figure
3

E.4
Visualizations for Intervention Extrapolation on Biological Data

In Figure
9
, we plot certain double-node intervention samples, both actual and sampled using our score-difference Langevin method in a UMAP
(
McInnes et al. 2018
)
plot. We plot the double-node interventions that
Zhang et al. 2023
plots in their double-node intervention extrapolation results that have more than
1000
samples for both single and double-node environments.

Figure 9
:
UMAP visualizations for double-node intervention extrapolation. Actual samples are in blue, generated samples are in orange.

We see across intervention pairs, score-based intervention extrapolation method leads to a sampling pattern that is relatively compliant with the actual double node interventional data. However, we observe that in some pairs, intervention effects are overestimated (particularly for CEBPE+SPI and ETS2+MAPK1).

E.5
Details of Score Functions Used in the Experiments

Score function of the linear Gaussian model.

For a Gaussian random vector
\mathbf{Y}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)
, the score function of
\mathbf{Y}
is given by

\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{Y}}(\mathbf{y})=-\Sigma^{-1}\cdot(\mathbf{y}-\mu)\ .

(311)

Using the linear model specified in (
102
), in environment
\mathcal{E}^{0}
,

\mathbf{Z}=(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}\cdot N_{i}\ .

(312)

Since
N_{i}
is a zero mean Gaussian random vector and
(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}
is a full rank matrix,
\mathbf{Z}
is a zero mean Gaussian random vector. Hence,

\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{z})=-\big[{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{Z})\big]^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}\ .

(313)

Note that the covariance of
\mathbf{Z}
has the following form

{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{Z})=(\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}\cdot{\rm diag}\big([\sigma_{1}^{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}^{2}]^{\top}\big)\cdot\big((\mathbf{I}_{n\times n}-\mathbf{A})^{-1}\big)^{\top}\ .

(314)

Similarly, the score function of
\mathbf{Z}
in
\mathcal{E}^{m}
is given by

\mathbf{s}^{m}(z)=-\big[{\rm Cov}(\mathbf{Z}^{m})\big]^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{z}\ ,\qquad\forall m\in[n]\ ,

(315)

in which
\mathbf{Z}^{m}
denotes the latent variables
\mathbf{Z}
in environment
\mathcal{E}^{m}
. Finally, by setting
f=\mathbf{G}
, Corollary
9
specifies the score differences of
\mathbf{X}
in terms of
\mathbf{Z}
under different environment pairs as

\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})=\big(\mathbf{G}^{{\dagger}}\big)^{\top}\cdot\big[\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{z})-\mathbf{s}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]\ .

(316)

Score estimation for a linear Gaussian model.

For all environments
\mathcal{E}^{m}\in\mathcal{E}
, given
n_{\rm s}
i.i.d. samples of
\mathbf{X}
, we first compute the sample covariance matrix denoted by
\hat{\Sigma}^{m}
. Then, we compute the sample precision matrix as

\hat{\Theta}^{m}\triangleq(\hat{\Sigma}^{m})^{{\dagger}}\ ,

(317)

which leads to the score function estimate given by

\hat{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})\triangleq-\hat{\Theta}^{m}\cdot\mathbf{x}\ .

(318)

Score function of the quadratic model.

Following (
16
), score functions
s^{m}
and
\tilde{s}^{m}
are decomposed as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log q_{\ell}(z_{\ell})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ ,

(319)

\displaystyle\mbox{and}\quad\tilde{\mathbf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log\tilde{q}_{\ell}(z_{\ell})+\sum_{i\neq\ell}\nabla_{\mathbf{z}}\log p_{i}(z_{i}\;|\;\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})\ .

(320)

For additive noise models, the terms in (
319
) and (
320
) have closed-form expressions. Specifically, using (
131
) and denoting the score functions of the noise terms
\{N_{i}:i\in[n]\}
by
\{r_{i}:i\in[n]\}
, we have

[\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{z})]_{i}=r_{i}(n_{i})-\sum_{j\in{\rm{ch}}(i)}\frac{\partial f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})}{\partial z_{i}}\cdot r_{j}(n_{j})\ .

(321)

Recall that we consider a quadratic latent model with

f_{i}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)})=\sqrt{\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{Q}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(i)}}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad N_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{i}^{2})\ ,

(322)

which implies

\frac{\partial f_{j}(\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)})}{\partial z_{i}}=\frac{[\mathbf{Q}_{j}]_{i}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}}{\sqrt{\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}^{\top}\cdot\mathbf{Q}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{{\rm{pa}}(j)}}}\ ,\quad\mbox{and}\quad r_{i}(n_{i})=-\frac{n_{i}}{\sigma_{i}^{2}}\ ,\quad\forall i\in[n]\ .

(323)

Components of the score functions
\mathbf{s}^{m}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{s}}^{m}
can be computed similarly. Subsequently, using Corollary
9
of Lemma
8
, we can compute the score differences of observed variables as follows.

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\Big[\big[J_{g}(\mathbf{z})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\big[\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{z})-\mathbf{s}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]\ ,

(324)

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\Big[\big[J_{g}(\mathbf{z})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\big[\mathbf{s}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\mathbf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]\ ,

(325)

\displaystyle\mathbf{s}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})-\tilde{\mathbf{s}}_{\mathbf{X}}^{m}(\mathbf{x})

\displaystyle=\Big[\big[J_{g}(\mathbf{z})\big]^{{\dagger}}\Big]^{\top}\cdot\big[\mathbf{s}^{m}(\mathbf{z})-\tilde{\mathbf{s}}^{m}(\mathbf{z})\big]\ .

(326)
